MacroVoices #560 Daniel Lacalle: “The European ER AH Situation…”59 min41 insights2026-11-25
▶ Watch on YouTube
After a market 'bloodbath', Tresis chief economist Daniel Lacalle (captioned 'Daniel Lay') argues AI capex is a dispersion risk not a bubble, sees oil settling near $70 WTI as the geopolitical premium erodes, calls the US dollar the 'least bad' fiat currency, says the euro's end game is debasement rather than break-up, and views the gold crash as a buying opportunity. Patrick Ceresna's market desk proposes a call-hedged short on March 2027 Euro FX futures and warns the selling in equities and gold is likely not over (S&P 6,900 test, gold 3,300 support).
AI capex: dispersion, not bubble · 6Oil: eroding geopolitical premium · 8Why the US-Europe gas spread persists · 4Dollar: least-bad fiat, Fed overreliance on rates · 6Euro area: stagnation and debasement end game · 8Gold crash as buying opportunity · 2Market desk: euro hedge and fragile tape · 7
AI capex: dispersion, not bubble 6
Daniel Lacalle argues the AI capex boom is not a bubble but a dispersion problem: some of the largest hyperscalers will be generating net cash in 2027 and can repay their debt, while other AI spenders will suffer declining margins or weak returns.
thesis🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: mediumhyperscalersartificial intelligence
So what: Favor selectivity (long cash-generative hyperscalers vs. weaker AI capex spenders) over blanket AI exposure or blanket shorts.
“when we look at the capital expenditure of the hyperscalers in the case of uh some of the largest ones, they're going to be generating net cash in 2027 and they will be able to repay their debt” ▶ 3:55
Lacalle says the market is currently unable to distinguish AI winners from losers and is selling all AI-linked names in unison, which should resolve into differentiation around the next earnings season.
prediction🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: mediumartificial intelligence
So what: Indiscriminate AI selling may create mispricings in higher-quality names ahead of next earnings season.
“The market right now is unable to decide which one is which and is basically just considering everything in in unison.” ▶ 5:02
Lacalle contends that because the AI bubble is debated constantly, the risk is already embedded in equity valuations and in the yields required on AI-related bonds, which argues against a classic bubble.
contrarian🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: mediumartificial intelligencecorporate bonds
So what: Suggests AI-related credit spreads already carry a risk premium; watch the next couple of months for clarification.
“it's not a bubble precisely because we're talking about it all the time” ▶ 5:29
Lacalle says data-center moratoria are a political rather than corporate risk: counting both capex overspend risk and moratoria-driven non-deployment double counts the risk, and blocked data centers will simply be built elsewhere.
framework🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: mediumdata centersIllinoisCaliforniaTexas
So what: Moratoria shift where AI investment, jobs and revenues land rather than whether they happen; states imposing them lose out.
“if you have on the one hand the concerns about the capital expenditure and on the other hand that capital expenditure is not going to be deployed then obviously you're double counting the risk” ▶ 6:36
If US jurisdictions block data centers, Lacalle expects them to be built in Mexico, North African countries, Asia and parts of Latin America that have ample power capacity and more benign regulation.
prediction🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: mediumMexicoNorth AfricaLatin AmericaAsiadata centers
So what: Potential beneficiaries are power-rich jurisdictions near the US (Mexico) and North Africa; watch for data-center investment flows there.
Lacalle expects choppy, volatile markets in both technology stocks and sovereign bonds until there is clarity on the Fed's rate path, with a December hike likely adding further volatility.
prediction🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: mediumFederal Reservesovereign bondstechnology
So what: The December Fed decision is the key near-term catalyst for both tech equities and bonds.
“If they increase rates in December, we may have further volatility.” ▶ 3:30
Oil: eroding geopolitical premium 8
Lacalle attributes the steep correction in energy markets to an erosion, but not disappearance, of the geopolitical risk premium, rejecting both a benign view and the 1973-style crisis some analysts predicted at the start of the Iran war.
thesis🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: mediumoilIran
So what: Some residual risk premium should stay in oil; neither tail scenario is the base case.
Gulf producers proved far more flexible than expected at recovering volumes and offsetting Iran-related supply risk, which Lacalle calls a significant surprise for many market participants.
causal🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: highGulf producersIranOPEC plus
So what: Spare-capacity response capped the oil spike; expect similar dampening on future disruptions.
Lacalle forecasts oil settling around $68-70 WTI, ruling out both $30 and $200 per barrel.
prediction🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: mediumWTIoil
So what: Provides a fair-value anchor for oil positioning and energy equity valuations.
“not being overly optimistic and thinking that we're going to see oil prices at $30 a barrel but certainly not 200” ▶ 12:30
Lacalle says a US-China-Russia commitment to guarantee supply of refined products is a big change, showing the two largest energy producers (US and Russia) will cooperate to provide security of supply to China.
thesis🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: mediumUnited StatesChinaRussiarefined products
So what: Great-power coordination on energy supply lowers tail risk for refined product prices.
China has the largest exposure to a Strait of Hormuz closure because 80% of the volumes through the strait go to Asia, which made it both a defender of and integral to the solution.
data_point🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: highChinaStrait of HormuzAsia
So what: China's incentive to keep Hormuz open makes it a stabilizing force on Gulf supply risk.
Asked how refined-product cooperation works if refineries keep being attacked, Lacalle concedes it is a vague promise unless Ukraine stops hitting Russian refineries, but argues the US can steer Ukraine away from refineries and China can deter Iran from striking Gulf refineries; he notes China is the second largest investor in Ukraine.
causal🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: lowUkraineRussiaChinaIranUnited Statesrefineries
So what: Continued refinery strikes would be the signal that the supply agreement is failing and crack spreads could reprice.
“if Ukraine continues to attack Russian refineries then there's no cooperation possible. You it's basically a vague promise.” ▶ 15:12
Lacalle reads point four of the agreement (non-nuclear proliferation) as implying China and Russia agree Iran will not get a nuclear weapon.
thesis🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: lowIranChinaRussia
So what: If correct, reduces a major tail risk embedded in the Middle East oil premium.
Lacalle names the United States and China as the big winners of the US-China-Russia energy agreement and the OPEC plus response to the Hormuz problem, while Europe sees limited consumer relief because heavy taxation dominates final gasoline prices.
thesis🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: mediumUnited StatesChinaOPEC plusEurope
So what: Lower crude helps US and Chinese demand more than European consumers; relative growth favors US/China.
Why the US-Europe gas spread persists 4
Lacalle expects European natural gas prices to stay well above pre-2022 levels because Ukraine-war disruptions persist, alternatives are not feeding through, and Germany's decision to shut down nuclear left Europe dependent on imported gas.
prediction🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: highnatural gasEuropeGermanynuclear
So what: Structurally high European energy costs remain a drag on European industry and growth.
“the misguided decision of Germany to shut down nuclear, the dependence on imported natural gas, those two things have not improved in any shape or form the situation for Europe” ▶ 13:36
Lacalle argues the long-predicted convergence of US and European natural gas prices via LNG exports will not happen because US producers always prioritize domestic consumption and transport costs are too high for clean arbitrage.
contrarian🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: highliquefied natural gasUnited StatesEuropenatural gas
So what: Don't bet on US natgas prices rising on export growth; the US-Europe spread can stay wide.
“I find it very very difficult to see natural gas prices in the United States going up because of higher exports to Europe”
US LNG is the marginal supplier to Europe, bought only after Algerian, Nigerian, Norwegian and Russian gas are exhausted, which lets sellers earn large margins on a small share of volumes.
framework🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: highliquefied natural gasAlgeriaNigeriaNorwayRussia
So what: US LNG exporters' profitability is tied to European supply shortfalls, not to setting the gas price.
Even after the liquefaction buildout, LNG is only about 15% of the total natural gas market, so it does not dictate the molecule's price.
data_point🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: highliquefied natural gasnatural gas
So what: Explains why regional gas price differentials persist despite LNG growth.
“it is only 15% of the total market” ▶ 21:05
Dollar: least-bad fiat, Fed overreliance on rates 6
Lacalle attributes the dollar index's push toward 104 to Fed rate hikes creating a 'vacuum effect' and to investors parking in dollars while taking profits on gold, tech and equities.
causal🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: highUS dollarFederal Reservegolddollar index
So what: Dollar strength is partly a by-product of de-risking; it reverses when profit-taking ends or the Fed eases.
Lacalle frames currency competition as a race to lose last: the dollar wins only as the least bad alternative to a euro with redenomination risk, a capital-controlled yuan, and a pound, Swiss franc and yen that lack the liquidity to replace it.
framework🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: highUS dollareuroyuanyenBritish pound
So what: Dollar reserve status holds despite debasement, which is consistent with long-term gold strength.
“the monetary world is not a world in which there is a battle to see who wins first but who loses first in the debasement of fiat currencies” ▶ 0:00
Lacalle doubts the dollar will strengthen much further because aggressive dollar appreciation historically triggered crises (yen crisis, French debt crisis, Asian financial crisis, Latin American tequila crisis), and nobody in central banking wants that.
prediction🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: mediumUS dollarcentral banks
So what: Upside in the DXY may be capped; watch EM and dollar-funding stress as a sign of overshoot.
“Will we see the US dollar strengthening a lot further? I doubt it.” ▶ 24:21
Lacalle says the Fed's tightening has engineered a massive contraction in financial conditions that is severely hurting countries that rely on dollar liquidity to finance their currencies and capex.
causal🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: highFederal ReserveUS dollaremerging markets
So what: Dollar-funded economies are vulnerable while Fed policy stays tight.
Lacalle argues today's Fed rate hikes have very limited impact on inflation and inflation expectations but large damage to families and small businesses, and the Fed should rely more on balance-sheet and liquidity tools instead.
contrarian🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: highFederal Reserve
So what: Further hikes raise growth/credit risk more than they lower inflation, increasing odds of an eventual pivot.
“today's world hiking rates has a very limited impact on inflation and on inflation expectations but a very large impact on the private sector” ▶ 27:55
Lacalle says the Fed cannot combat inflation unless fiscal policy is restrictive, because the Fed cannot print anything the government has not printed earlier.
framework🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: highFederal Reservefiscal policy
So what: Fiscal deficits, not Fed rhetoric, are the key inflation variable to watch.
“the Fed cannot print anything that the government has not printed earlier” ▶ 28:45
Euro area: stagnation and debasement end game 8
Lacalle says the EU's interventionist energy, industrial, agriculture, technology and finance policies have failed, and the euro area is stagnating despite negative rates, massive ECB stimulus and the Next Generation fund.
thesis🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: highEuropean UnionEuropean Central BankNext Generation fund
So what: Supports a structural underweight of euro-area growth assets versus US and China.
Lacalle says the EU lacks a technological, energy or financial-sector advantage, though its banks have navigated the situation very well.
thesis🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: mediumEuropean UnionEuropean banks
So what: European banks may be the relative bright spot within a weak European macro picture.
Lacalle sees hope in voters and businesses pushing back, which could bring political change that starts unwinding Europe's bureaucratic, regulatory and tax burdens.
prediction🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: exploratoryEuropean Union
So what: Political change toward deregulation would be the upside catalyst for European assets.
Responding to Louis Gave's view that France or Italy may be forced out of the euro, Lacalle calls redenomination risk real but very unlikely because TARGET2 imbalances mean a French exit would send Germany into depression and devastate bank and pension balance sheets.
contrarian🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: highFranceItalyGermanyLouis GavTARGET2euro
So what: Euro break-up trades are low probability; position for depreciation instead.
“France leaving the euro means Germany enters into a depression.” ▶ 34:45
Lacalle estimates a new French franc would be worth about 50 cents on the euro at best, effectively slashing French pensions by 70-80%, and a euro break-up would also hit Japanese pension funds that have bought European debt for yield.
data_point🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: mediumFranceJapanpension funds
So what: Contagion from a euro break-up would extend to Japan, which is one more reason policymakers will avoid it.
Lacalle calls the euro a 'bull market project' that works when economies grow but not in stagnation, comparing the euro area to the Hotel California: you can check out but never leave.
framework🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: higheuroeuro area
So what: Euro stress rises in downturns; expect policy to absorb it through the currency.
“the euro is a bull market project” ▶ 37:53
Lacalle believes the euro's most probable end game is a much more devalued, debased currency, the least damaging path though one that raises euro-area inflation expectations; he also expects the UK to be forced back toward the EU despite the referendum.
prediction🗣 Daniel Lacalleconviction: higheuroEuropean Central BankUK
So what: Structural bearish EUR thesis; favors real assets over euro-denominated bonds.

Wealthion 1 episode(s) · 41 insights

Why the Oil Supply Crunch Isn't Over Yet | Rory Johnston53 min41 insights2026-11-25
▶ Watch on YouTube
Rory Johnson (Commodity Context) argues the late-2026 oil selloff reflects lagged October Hormuz flows and washed-out positioning, while markets are complacent about a ~25% daily risk of losing 5 mb/d of Hormuz supply with inventories below five-year lows. Structurally the market is in an OPEC-driven glut that would push Brent toward $40 absent war, but diesel/refining tightness, a jurisdictional premium for safe assets, and China's hidden stock draws (and likely restocking) reshape the picture.

Late-November oil selloff and positioning 7
Crude fell roughly 18% on Nymex and 21% on Brent over the month to late November 2026 after peaking near $115-118 Brent just before the US midterm elections.
data_point🗣 host (Maggie Lake)conviction: highBrentNymexcrude oil
So what: Sizes the unwind of the pre-midterm war premium; context for mean-reversion trades.
Rory Johnson attributes the pre-midterm spike to market expectations that Trump would escalate the war with Iran before the elections; when that did not happen the premium unwound and momentum selling followed.
causal🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumTrumpIranBrent
So what: Event-driven escalation premia can deflate fast once the catalyst date passes.
Net speculative positioning in crude (CFTC and ICE Commitments of Traders) has fallen to its lowest level since the rollout after the June-July Memorandum of Understanding, leaving the market oversold and primed for a short-term bounce.
thesis🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumCFTCICEcrude oil
So what: Washed-out positioning argues against chasing shorts; watch COT data for a squeeze setup.
“right now, just based on positioning, we are oversold. And I think we'll see some kind of surge here” ▶ 1:34
October 2026 saw the largest volume of oil transiting the Strait of Hormuz since the war began, even as Iranian attacks on shipping increased.
data_point🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highStrait of HormuzIran
So what: Explains the bearish physical turn of November despite headline risk.
Brent hit almost $120 at end-October while fundamentals were probably in the first global monthly surplus since the war started, which Johnson explains as a roughly month-and-a-half lag between Hormuz flows and their arrival in physical markets.
framework🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumBrentStrait of Hormuz
So what: Price and fundamentals can be out of phase by ~6 weeks; trade the lagged arrival of barrels, not the transit headline.
Johnson's call: if the recent decline in Hormuz transits persists through end-November and into December, physical oil markets will strengthen fundamentally as those supply shortfalls land.
prediction🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumStrait of Hormuzcrude oil
So what: Sets up a December physical tightening; bullish for prompt spreads if transit data stays weak.
The consensus narrative that Middle East supply has 'fully recovered from the Hormuz shock' is stale, based on headlines three to four weeks old, according to Johnson.
contrarian🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumStrait of HormuzMiddle East
So what: Contrarian to the bearish consensus; supports leaning long into the oversold tape.
Reading a high-frequency, distrusted oil market 6
In a market where every data point has counter-data and nobody trusts anything, Johnson says the only trustworthy signals are realized physical market indicators such as term structure and North Sea front-line spreads.
framework🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highNorth SeaBrent
So what: Prioritize physical spreads and curve shape over contested flow/tanker data.
“the only thing people can really trust are these physical realized market signals” ▶ 4:08
Physical market indicators are short-sighted: a surplus visible at a given moment reflects prior flows, so they can mislead on the forward trajectory.
framework🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highcrude oil
So what: Combine physical signals with forward flow estimates; don't extrapolate a single month's surplus.
Normal oil markets swing by about 1-2% of global balances (a 1 mb/d deficit to 2 mb/d surplus) and evolve slowly, whereas Hormuz transits are now changing by about 4.5 million barrels per day month to month, something not seen outside early COVID.
data_point🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highStrait of HormuzCOVID
So what: Monthly data with a two-month lag is obsolete; volatility regime justifies higher implied vols and faster rebalancing.
“Every month we see changes of 4.5 million barrels per day. We've never seen anything like this” ▶ 7:08
Tanker-tracking data for Hormuz popular on X is less accurate than markets are used to because shipping behavior keeps changing with the threat environment, creating false signals.
causal🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumStrait of HormuzX
So what: Discount social-media tanker counts; noisy data amplifies sentiment swings.
Johnson's key monitoring rule: sustained contango on both the physical Brent curve and the futures curve for more than a month or two would signal the crisis is genuinely easing; anything shorter is noise.
framework🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highBrentcontango
So what: A concrete trigger for flipping from tight-market to surplus positioning.
“if we see contango on the physical curve and then contango on the futures curve for, say, more than a month or two, that's a sign that things are really starting to ease up” ▶ 50:21
In early November the front of the physical Brent curve and the CFD curve flipped into contango (spot North Sea Brent below two-month forward) even while futures stayed inverted, the most bearish signal Johnson saw; over the past three weeks it has reverted to backwardation, mirroring the June-July 'mini-glut'.
data_point🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumBrentNorth SeaCFD
So what: Transient contango episodes reflect clustered tanker arrivals, not a durable glut; fade them unless sustained.
Complacency vs. tail risk in Hormuz supply 6
Johnson estimates current Hormuz flows at just over 10 million barrels per day versus about 20 million before the war.
data_point🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumStrait of Hormuz
So what: Half of pre-war flow remains offline; any further loss hits a thin buffer.
September 2026 showed about a 1 mb/d global deficit, but against a no-war baseline of a 3 mb/d surplus the market is effectively 4 mb/d short of where it would have been.
framework🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumcrude oil
So what: Judge balances relative to the counterfactual, not to zero; a 'small' deficit is a large shock.
“the normal scenario without the war would be 3 million barrels a day of surplus. So we're still 4 million barrels a day short” ▶ 11:08
Global total oil inventories are well below five-year lows after hundreds of millions of barrels were drawn during the war, with commercial and strategic stocks even lower.
data_point🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highSPRinventories
So what: Low stocks mean surpluses first refill tanks rather than crush prices; supports a price floor.
Johnson sees roughly a 25% risk that on any given day the market could lose 5 million barrels per day of Hormuz supply via Iranian escalation or a US strike, which he calls unprecedented and underpriced.
prediction🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highIranTrumpStrait of Hormuz
So what: Fat right tail argues for cheap upside optionality (calls) in crude.
“the fact that we have a 25% risk that on any given day we could lose 5 million barrels of global oil supply per day, we've never lived in an oil market like this” ▶ 51:05
A Libyan pipeline outage costing 350,000 barrels per day, which would have dominated markets for weeks in 2019, is now treated as a rounding error, illustrating how risk perception has shifted.
anecdote🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumLibyaLibyan National Oil Company
So what: Mid-sized outages elsewhere are being ignored; cumulative small disruptions may be underpriced.
Johnson views the oil market as structurally bearish if the Iran war ends, but sees no end point in sight, so the current price complacency is unjustified.
thesis🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumIrancrude oil
So what: Base case bearish only conditional on a peace that isn't visible; position for persistence of risk.
“Structurally, this is a bear market if it ever ends. But there doesn't seem to be any end point in sight.” ▶ 11:53
OPEC policy and the structural glut 8
Johnson's pre-war view was so bearish he expected Brent could be around $40 per barrel at this point in 2026 absent the Iran war.
data_point🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highBrentIran
So what: Reveals how large the underlying glut is once the war premium is removed.
“I thought that at this time of year, in a world where there was no war with Iran, Brent might be worth $40 a barrel” ▶ 10:29
The structural surplus is largely OPEC's making: from October 2022 to early 2024 OPEC+ cut about 6 mb/d (two-thirds of the COVID cut) while prices were above $90, including Saudi Arabia's 'lollipop' unilateral cut in mid-2023, which suppressed demand growth and subsidized US shale growth.
causal🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highOPECSaudi ArabiaU.S. shale
So what: Defending high prices with spare capacity builds a glut that eventually must be released.
Johnson compares OPEC's 2022-2024 cuts to Saudi Arabia's pre-1986 strategy, when North Sea growth let British output exceed Saudi production until the kingdom opened the taps, producing a lost decade for oil in the 1980s.
framework🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumOPECSaudi ArabiaNorth Sea
So what: Historical analog for a price-war unwind of hoarded spare capacity.
OPEC began rapidly unwinding its cuts in early 2025, putting the market on a glut trajectory designed to force out higher-cost supply, chiefly US shale.
causal🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highOPECU.S. shale
So what: OPEC market-share strategy targets short-cycle shale; bearish for shale producers when war risk fades.
Long-cycle supply growth from Canada, Guyana, Brazil and Argentina (5-10 year deepwater/oil sands cycles) won't respond to short price drops, leaving US shale as the swing producer that must be rationalized.
framework🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highCanadaGuyanaBrazilArgentinaU.S. shale
So what: Price downside concentrates on shale activity; long-cycle producers keep growing regardless.
Dallas Fed cost curves put the average new-well breakeven in the Permian Basin around $60 per barrel; Johnson saw $40 not as a long-run price but as the level needed to make US shale contract and rebalance the market.
data_point🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highFederal Reserve in DallasPermian BasinU.S. shale
So what: Sub-$60 sustained prices would cut Permian drilling; $40 is a temporary clearing price, not equilibrium.
“$40 is not a long-term sustainable price for oil. That was the price that we needed to rationalize enough U.S. supply” ▶ 24:50
Because triple-digit war prices let US shale keep growing instead of contracting, the structural glut has worsened; absent the war, the 2027 surplus should be larger than what was expected for 2026 given another year of OPEC+ growth and weak demand.
prediction🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumU.S. shaleOPECIran
So what: A peace deal in 2027 could trigger a sharper price collapse than consensus expects.
Johnson expects no flood of surplus as long as Middle East supply risk persists, so his base outcome for 2027 hinges entirely on the course of the Iran war.
thesis🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumIranMiddle East
So what: Treat 2027 oil as a binary on war resolution; size positions accordingly.
Jurisdictional premium and refining/diesel squeeze 7
Oil production and reserves located away from the Hormuz conflict zone, such as in the US and Canada, now carry higher implicit option value, a jurisdictional premium for equities holding such assets.
thesis🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highUnited StatesCanadaStrait of Hormuz
So what: Favor North American producers' reserve valuations over Gulf-exposed assets.
“if you have oil production that's not in an area where there are no missiles flying, it's more valuable today” ▶ 16:16
Western refineries once seen as stranded legacy assets have become far more valuable because drones and asymmetric threats make refineries in the Persian Gulf and Russia easy, high-value targets.
contrarian🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highrefineriesPersian GulfRussia
So what: Long safe-jurisdiction refiners; refining capacity scarcity is structural.
Russia, the world's third-largest refining center, has had up to half its refining capacity offline at points this year, a Hormuz-scale shock to refining.
data_point🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highRussiarefining
So what: Explains outsized product cracks versus crude.
While crude peaked near $120, diesel reached almost $230 per barrel in New York Harbor; diesel cracks went from about $20 pre-COVID (about $30 pre-crisis) to $120 at the September peak and are now about $65, still roughly three times normal.
data_point🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highdieselNew York Harbor
So what: Diesel tightness persists after crude eases; middle distillate cracks remain the cleaner bullish trade.
EV adoption mainly displaces gasoline, so refiners must recover margins from diesel, while the growth of light US shale crude yields more gasoline and less diesel; both trends structurally push diesel cracks higher.
causal🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highdieselgasolineelectric vehiclesU.S. shale
So what: Structural case for elevated diesel spreads and refiners with high distillate yields.
Farm-state voters were hit hard by diesel prices ahead of the midterms, with agricultural districts telling Johnson that falling gasoline prices did not offset diesel pain affecting all transport and farm equipment.
anecdote🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumdieselmidterm elections
So what: Diesel is a political pressure point that could drive policy intervention.
AI-driven energy demand is mostly a natural gas story (peaker plants, local turbines), and has reversed the long trend of flat aggregate energy demand; the debate is now about the degree of growth, not direction.
thesis🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumartificial intelligencenatural gas
So what: Supports gas-fired power demand regardless of AI equity volatility.
China's hidden stock draw and oil demand 7
China was the biggest surprise of 2026: it cut crude imports by about 5.5 mb/d, almost half of its total, between February and June-July, which is why Johnson's March call for possible $200 oil missed.
data_point🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highChinacrude oil
So what: China acted as a de facto swing buyer; its import behavior now caps and floors prices.
“China was the biggest surprise this year” ▶ 33:32
China cut refining by almost 3 mb/d, the steepest in its history, yet trucking activity rose 3.5% year on year while apparent diesel demand fell 20%, implying large unreported draws of underground crude reserves and product (especially diesel) stocks rather than true demand destruction.
causal🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumChinadiesel
So what: Chinese 'demand destruction' was largely inventory draw, so it will be reversed by restocking.
China never acknowledged SPR releases; crude stock estimates rely on satellite readings of floating-roof tanks, which cannot see underground strategic storage or most product storage, making China an inventory black box.
framework🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highChinaSPR
So what: Treat Chinese inventory estimates with wide error bars; flows can swing without warning.
Johnson estimates actual Chinese demand loss at around 1 mb/d, not 5 mb/d-plus, and calls claims that the episode proved the end of the oil era misleading.
contrarian🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumChina
So what: Pushes back on peak-oil-demand narratives built on 2026 Chinese import data.
China's EV-driven gasoline displacement has accelerated from about 100-200 kb/d two to three years ago to 500-600 kb/d this year, making Chinese oil demand stagnant at best and possibly negative going forward.
data_point🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: highChinaelectric vehicles
So what: Loss of the historic engine of half of global demand growth is a structural bearish factor.
Johnson's theories for why China drew stocks: protecting domestic price caps, shielding its Asian and European export markets from a Hormuz-driven recession, and possibly stress-testing an energy-security blanket for a Malacca Strait blockade in a Taiwan scenario.
framework🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: exploratoryChinaTaiwanStraits of Malacca
So what: China demonstrated geopolitical leverage over oil prices larger than the IEA's 400 million barrel coordinated release.
Johnson expects China to restock crude and products if prices fall to $50-60, and with commercial inventories very low globally, a return to surplus would first push the front of the futures curve into contango before a true glut and price crush.
prediction🗣 Rory Johnsonconviction: mediumChinacontangoinventories
So what: Chinese restocking creates a soft floor in the $50-60 range; watch curve shape for glut confirmation.

Top Traders Unplugged 1 episode(s) · 39 insights

When AI Builds Your Portfolio, Who Checks the Work? ft. Rob Carver | Systematic Investor | Ep. 42774 min39 insights2026-11-25
▶ Watch on YouTube
Rob Carver and host Niels review a November selloff (crude -20%, Nikkei -10%) where CTAs outperformed equities, then argue AI tools risk crowding (Bloomberg AskB) and overfit backtests via look-ahead bias and 'marking your own homework'; Carver advocates strict add-only backtests, global robust parameters, and diversified trend funds as the default hedge.
AI-driven crowding and alpha decay · 5Market and CTA performance snapshot · 7Industry flows and news · 6Strategy fitting and overfitting discipline · 9Limits and uses of AI in strategy design · 6Pairing trend following with portfolios · 6
AI-driven crowding and alpha decay 5
If many funds use the same commercial AI (e.g., Bloomberg's AskB) and get the same answers, positions will crowd; crowding hurts relative value and carry trades most, trend following much less.
causal🗣 Rob Carverconviction: mediumBloombergAskBAIrelative valuecarrytrend following
So what: Expect AI-standardized advice to raise crowding risk in RV/carry books; trend is relatively insulated.
“some trades do very badly when they get crowded particularly relative value trades. It's not quite so true with trend following but but certainly carry and relative value definitely do worse if there's crowding” ▶ 19:15
Crowding risk from shared AI tools is concentrated among small-to-midsize hedge funds (e.g., $50M AUM) that cannot build proprietary AI, unlike giants like Goldman Sachs that will have their own.
thesis🗣 Rob Carverconviction: mediumGoldman SachsBloomberghedge funds
So what: Smaller-fund flows may become more correlated as they converge on the same vendor AI outputs.
Research described on another podcast found that retail investors given a broker-provided AI were nudged to stop active trading and buy index ETFs, showing AI advice is more persuasive than identical conventional advice — crowding, but in a beneficial direction.
data_point🗣 Rob Carverconviction: mediumAIindex ETFsretail investors
So what: AI advisers could accelerate retail flows into passive index products.
Position crowding does not require AI: many were long the AI trade and there have been public blowups plus rumored pod blowups; AI may simply make factor crowding worse.
thesis🗣 Rob Carverconviction: mediumAI tradepodsmulti-manager hedge funds
So what: Monitor pod/multi-manager crowding in AI-related factor exposure as a source of unwind risk.
Niels proposed testing whether AskB recommends a common optimal allocation across global equities, global bonds, trend following and gold, on the view that investors may increasingly adopt whatever AI tells them.
thesis🗣 host (Niels)conviction: exploratoryAskBBloomberggoldtrend following
So what: If AI recommendations converge, allocation flows into products like trend following could follow AI defaults.

So what: Blending trend with other signals smooths results relative to pure trend indices.

framework🗣 Rob Carverconviction: mediumCTAdiversificationfactor exposure
So what: Check whether a CTA's book is concentrated in one factor; diversified books are less exposed to single-factor reversals.

Strategy fitting and overfitting discipline 9
For holding periods of about a month or more, continually tweaking one algorithm is at best marginally additive and at worst overfitting; Rob Carver has recalibrated his own system only about twice in 12 years against a backtest back to 1970.
framework🗣 Rob Carverconviction: highRob Carveroverfittingbacktesting
So what: Frequent retuning of slow strategies is a red flag for overfitting in managers or one's own process.
Continuous refitting is appropriate for high-frequency trading because HFT generates thousands of data points a day and suffers faster alpha decay.
framework🗣 Rob Carverconviction: highhigh frequency tradingalpha decay
So what: Refit cadence should match data density and decay speed of the strategy.
'Methodology shopping' — trying many fitting/optimization approaches and picking the best in-sample — is itself a meta-level form of in-sample fitting.
framework🗣 Rob Carverconviction: highmethodology shoppingoverfitting
So what: Discount backtests from researchers who iterate over optimization methods.
“methodology shopping where you basically hunt out the best sort of fitting strategy that gives you the highest performance sort of in sample” ▶ 28:27
Niels argued research still matters: genuinely new, complementary ideas are worthwhile, whereas parameter tweaks to the same methodology are not.
thesis🗣 host (Niels)conviction: mediumresearchCTA
So what: Judge research departments by new diversifying ideas, not parameter churn.
A Resolve Asset Management paper on idiosyncratic market effects in time-series momentum (50 futures, 1950-2026) found per-market nonlinear response curves fitted pre-1995 gave a small but significant out-of-sample improvement over ~30 years.
data_point🗣 Rob Carverconviction: mediumResolve Asset ManagementAndrew Butlertime series momentum
So what: Market-specific nonlinear sizing has measurable but modest edge.
Response curves differ by momentum speed: slow momentum is near linear, while fast momentum shows S-shaped tails where very strong trends can mean revert; Carver uses a linear response capped at extremes.
framework🗣 Rob Carverconviction: highmomentumresponse curvemean reversion
So what: Cap position size on extreme fast-momentum signals to avoid tail mean reversion.
Rob Carver would not implement per-market nonlinear fits because the gain is small relative to the complexity and simple in-sample/out-of-sample splits cannot detect evolving curve shapes.
thesis🗣 Rob Carverconviction: highRob CarverResolve Asset Management
So what: Prefer robust global models; treat small per-market gains skeptically.
Both Niels's firm (moving from per-market settings to global settings over the last 20 years) and AHL (moving to a robust global setting around 2011) shifted toward simpler global parameters for robustness.
data_point🗣 host (Niels)conviction: highAHLCTAglobal settings
So what: Industry best practice among leading CTAs favors global, robust parameters over per-market fitting.
Rejecting a tested strategy idea before adding it to the backtest is itself in-sample fitting, because it uses information unavailable at the backtest's start; Carver advocates an 'add-on' backtest where ideas can be added but never deleted (only down-weighted).
contrarian🗣 Rob Carverconviction: highbacktestingin-sample fittingRob Carver
So what: Backtest performance of most strategies is inflated by discarded ideas — haircut reported Sharpe accordingly.
“You can only add new ideas to it. Uh you can't delete once.” ▶ 1:09:59
Limits and uses of AI in strategy design 6
Rob Carver argues AI cannot generate genuinely original trading ideas, but can surface papers and prompt human ideas, akin to browsing library shelves — while hallucinating references that must be checked.
thesis🗣 Rob Carverconviction: highAILLM
So what: Use LLMs for literature discovery, not alpha generation.
“if you're going to be relying on AI to come up with original ideas, then you know, you might you might end up picking up an idea from elsewhere you weren't aware of, but it isn't going to be an it's not going to be come up with genuinely original ideas” ▶ 32:32
LLM-based backtests (e.g., the 'self-driving portfolio' agentic asset-allocation paper) suffer look-ahead bias because commercial LLMs are trained on data up to their cutoff, and nobody yet refits LLM weights each year as a proper walk-forward test would require.
contrarian🗣 Rob Carverconviction: highLLMagentic AIself-driving portfolioAltbridge
So what: Treat AI-agent portfolio backtests as contaminated by data leakage until proven otherwise.
Using an LLM both to build a strategy and to judge whether it works, including writing its own backtest code, is 'marking your own homework'.
framework🗣 Rob Carverconviction: highLLMAI codebacktesting
So what: Demand independent, human-written backtest frameworks when evaluating AI-built strategies.
“I call it marking your own homework” ▶ 1:00:48
AI is useful for idea prompting, summarizing research, finding data sources, writing small (~50-line) verifiable non-core code, and suggesting bugs — in one case 3 of 4 AI code suggestions were good but the 4th would have destroyed the code.
data_point🗣 Rob Carverconviction: highAIcode review
So what: Keep AI out of core execution code and never let AI write and check code unsupervised.
To counter AI's forward-looking knowledge, a backtest of trend following should simultaneously test the opposite hypothesis (mean reversion) because in 1970 you would not have known trend worked.
framework🗣 Rob Carverconviction: hightrend followingmean reversionbacktestinginformation leakage
So what: Robust research tests competing hypotheses side by side to neutralize hindsight embedded in AI or researchers.
Carver says the standard strategy-design loop (economic rationale, signal design, parameter selection, statistical validation, portfolio integration) is how nearly everyone works, and AI will make its embedded in-sample selection problem worse; his book after the one due December 1 will cover strict backtesting.
prediction🗣 Rob Carverconviction: mediumHandlesmeisterAIbacktesting
So what: Expect more overfit AI-assisted strategies; rigor in backtest design becomes a differentiator.
Pairing trend following with portfolios 6
In a linear correlation framework, adding trend following in the same asset class you already hold (e.g., equity trend on an equity-heavy portfolio) adds beta, since trend has positive beta to assets that rise over time; you'd prefer a CTA with less allocation to that asset class.
contrarian🗣 Rob Carverconviction: hightrend followingCTAequity beta
So what: An equity-heavy investor should seek CTAs underweight equity trend, not overweight it.
Trend following's tail-hedge splits into a guaranteed mechanical component (going short a falling market) and an empirical 'crisis alpha' component that is not guaranteed — e.g., 2008's protracted selloff was ideal while last year's tariff tantrum sharp drop-and-reversal was awful for medium-speed trend.
framework🗣 Rob Carverconviction: highcrisis alphatrend following2008 financial crisistariff tantrum
So what: Don't count on crisis alpha in V-shaped crashes; it works best in protracted selloffs.
Regardless of the underlying portfolio, investors are probably better off buying a diversified trend-following fund across all asset classes rather than one matching their concentrated exposure.
thesis🗣 Rob Carverconviction: hightrend followingCTAdiversification
So what: Allocate to diversified CTAs as the default hedge for any portfolio.
“irrespective of what your underlying portfolio is you're probably better off buying just a diversified trend following fund that allocates across all asset classes” ▶ 49:27
Niels cited research (possibly Alex Grman at ISAM) that during equity crises the most consistently profitable CTA sectors are commodities, not the crisis sector — e.g., CTAs got short energies about a month before the 'co' (COVID-era) equity collapse, and positioned in energy before this year's March 1 war start.
data_point🗣 host (Niels)conviction: mediumISAMAlex GrmancommoditiesenergyCOVID
So what: Commodity trend exposure is a key driver of CTA crisis performance; avoid CTAs without broad commodity coverage.
For equity hedging via trend, a 20% allocation is too small to hedge; bonds can be hedged more efficiently because bond trend portfolios can be levered up.
framework🗣 Rob Carverconviction: mediumtrend followingbondsequitiesleverage
So what: Size trend allocations larger than 20% if the goal is a real equity hedge.
Without futures access, leveraged ETFs (e.g., 2x bond ETFs) are a crude substitute for trend exposure in equities and bonds, but suffer higher cost and daily-reset issues; futures are better for larger accounts.
framework🗣 Rob Carverconviction: mediumleveraged ETFsfuturesS&PNASDAQ
So what: Retail trend implementers should prefer futures where possible.

AI adoption speed and diffusion lag 8
Elad Gil says two AI labs, Anthropic and OpenAI, went from essentially nothing to roughly a trillion dollars of market cap in about five years, one of the most accelerated technology adoption runs in history.
data_point🗣 Elad Gilconviction: highAnthropicOpenAI
So what: Sets the reference class that private-market investors are now (mis)using to price every AI startup.
“over the last four or five years, we've seen two companies go from zero to a trillion dollars of market cap from nothing” ▶ 8:07
Gil notes the leading labs are approaching around $100 billion of revenue each within five years, which he frames as real adoption rather than an overinflated price.
data_point🗣 Elad Gilconviction: highAnthropicOpenAI
So what: Supports the view that lab valuations are backed by revenue, unlike most of the startups priced off them.
“they're also hitting like 100 billion of revenue in five years. Like think of that. That's insane. That's real adoption.” ▶ 11:35
Gil describes a diffusion gradient: AI labs are 6-9 months ahead of the average Silicon Valley engineer via internal models, Silicon Valley engineers are 6-12 months ahead of engineers elsewhere, and ordinary users (e.g. in Paris) still use ChatGPT for basic doc cleanup.
framework🗣 Elad Gilconviction: highAI labsChatGPT
So what: Most of AI's economic impact has not yet diffused; adoption-driven revenue upside outside the Bay Area is still ahead.
“the labs are say six to nine months ahead of the average engineer here because they have internal models” ▶ 8:52
Gil argues one year of AI progress now equals roughly three to five years of a normal technology cycle, so companies rise and fall unusually fast.
framework🗣 Elad Gilconviction: highAI
So what: Compresses holding-period assumptions; competitive positions in AI should be re-underwritten far more often.
“every year an AI is like three to five years in a normal cycle” ▶ 10:06
Gil points out that a year earlier an MIT report claimed 90% of AI was useless and articles argued AI ROI did not justify more investment, a view the subsequent year of progress overturned.
contrarian🗣 Elad Gilconviction: mediumMITAI
So what: Consensus skepticism on AI ROI has recently been wrong; be wary of fading the trend on ROI narratives alone.
Gil frames the current AI moment as 'punctuated equilibrium': a burst of new companies that will typically be followed by consolidation in which only some companies and use cases survive.
framework🗣 Elad Gilconviction: highAI
So what: Expect a shakeout; position for survivors and acquirers rather than the long tail of new entrants.
Gil predicts the next 12 months will bring even more AI progress than the last 12, with diffusion accelerating and first real impacts beyond language and online use cases, though full diffusion will take about a decade.
prediction🗣 Elad Gilconviction: highAI
So what: Supports continued spend and capability growth; watch for AI impact in physical-world sectors as the next leg.
“I think we'll see potentially even more progress in the next year than we saw in the last year” ▶ 1:11:38
Gil expects semiconductor design, chip firmware and kernel writing to become largely AI-driven, shortening chip design cycles and making migration onto new chipsets dramatically faster.
prediction🗣 Elad Gilconviction: mediumsemiconductorschipskernels

“you're able to migrate onto new chipsets dramatically faster than they ever have been” ▶ 1:12:11
Startup valuations and the 'sell now' case 9
Gil says AI pre-seed rounds that were about $1 million at a $10 million valuation in 2022 are now $10-20 million at $100-200 million valuations for three people with an idea, often with tranched rounds at $500 million raised simultaneously.
data_point🗣 Elad Gilconviction: highAI startupsOpenAI
So what: Early-stage AI entry prices are ~10x higher than in 2022, sharply raising the bar for venture returns.
“They're raising $10 million, $20 million at a$100 to $200 million valuation with like three people in an idea.” ▶ 0:16
Gil's rule of thumb is that companies eventually trade at about 10x high-margin (70-80%) software revenue, so every $1 billion of valuation implies $50-100 million of fast-growing revenue; four-month-old companies at $1 billion only work if some become $100 billion-$1 trillion companies.
framework🗣 Elad Gilconviction: highAI startups
So what: Use revenue-multiple back-solving to judge whether a private AI mark is plausible.
“every billion dollars of market cap was eventually going to be $50 to $100 million of revenue growing rapidly” ▶ 14:28
Gil says many AI startups are being priced as if they were Anthropic, with investors extrapolating large market size into fast adoption speed, which are two different things.
contrarian🗣 Elad Gilconviction: highAnthropicAI startups
So what: Discount startups whose valuations assume lab-like adoption curves in slower-moving markets.
“I think a lot of um companies are being treated as if they're anthropic.” ▶ 11:16
Gil argues most AI companies should consider exiting within the next 12-18 months because many are at the most valuable point they will ever be, and boards should formally discuss selling every six months given AI's clock speed.
thesis🗣 Elad Gilconviction: highAI startupsBen Horus
So what: Expect elevated AI M&A; for holders of private AI stakes, near-term liquidity may beat holding.
“for many companies in AI, that's probably right around now.” ▶ 55:53
Gil cites the dot-com precedent: roughly 2,000 internet companies IPO'd in the late 1990s and perhaps two dozen still exist; many that could have sold but did not went to zero.
data_point🗣 Elad Gilconviction: highdot-com era
So what: Historical base rate argues most current AI winners-of-the-moment will not endure independently.
“Many of the ones that could have sold but didn't went to zero.” ▶ 0:26
Gil says companies from the 2020-2021 valuation upswing that turned down offers of hundreds of millions are now fire-saling or shutting down.
anecdote🗣 Elad Gilconviction: high2021 vintage startups
So what: Concrete evidence that refusing peak-cycle offers often destroys value; relevant for 2021-vintage portfolios.
Gil lists exit triggers: being paid years ahead of fundamentals, a large competitor entering and threatening growth (since buyers extrapolate growth rates), founder burnout or conflict, or a bigger-impact home at OpenAI, Anthropic or Google.
framework🗣 Elad Gilconviction: mediumOpenAIAnthropicGoogle
So what: A checklist for spotting AI startups likely to sell soon, e.g. those facing a lab entering their market.

North Korea Binance Hack Laundering 6
Elliptic has mapped the trail of North Korea's roughly $6.4 billion drain from Binance, with 44% frozen, a record, but most of that came from the very earliest actions.
data_point🗣 Taylor Monahanconviction: highBinanceNorth KoreaElliptic
So what: Recovery prospects decay fast after the first hours; recovery estimates should not be extrapolated from early freezes.
“it's the majority of that is those very very early actions.” ▶ 51:54
Taylor Monahan says she stopped tracing because the effort is no longer worth the reward: in the Bybit case (captioned Vivbit), after strong early freezes, 16 people traced 24/7 for four months for small recoveries.
contrarian🗣 Taylor Monahanconviction: highVivbit
So what: Late-stage tracing of mega-hacks has diminishing returns; meaningful recovery must be frontloaded in the first ~48 hours.
“we're past the point where the effort is worth the reward” ▶ 52:05
The launderers use about 27,000 wallets and much more automation, scripting and probably AI than before, but scripted/AI-driven flows are easier for AI to trace than unpredictable humans.
causal🗣 Taylor Monahanconviction: mediumNorth Korea
So what: Automation by attackers improves chain-analytics effectiveness; tailwind for AI-based tracing vendors like Elliptic and TRM.
“the AI can trace the AI. The scripts can trace the scripts.” ▶ 59:53
Freezes depend on anticipating flows like ETH to USDT then bridging USDT to Tron, pre-prepping Tether or bridges, and often law-enforcement or Binance backing to assume liability, while THORChain won't freeze anything.
framework🗣 Taylor Monahanconviction: highTetherTronthor chainBinanceUSDT

“thor chain won't do anything.” ▶ 54:46

data_point🗣 Taylor Monahanconviction: highNorth KoreaKim Jong-unETHBitcoin

Chat With Traders 1 episode(s) · 30 insights

The Psychology of Losing: Why Winners Add & Losers Hope · Tom Hougaard & David Capablanca76 min30 insights2026-11-25
▶ Watch on YouTube
Recorded at a live Chat with Traders event in LA. Tom Hougaard argues that most of his money comes from adding to winners on trend days, rejects fixed targets, and calls prop firms a fee business with a 98-99% failure rate. Small-cap short seller David Capablanca explains how he survives by excluding whole categories (nano floats, Chinese stocks), describes halt and trade-bust tail risks, and lays out graded, seasonal position sizing. Much of the talk is motivational and biographical.
Prop firm economics and failure rates · 3Small-cap shorting: cut out whole categories · 7Trade busts and exchange tail risk · 3Adding to winners and trend days · 6Targets, exits and pain avoidance · 5Position sizing, scaling and seasonality · 6
Prop firm economics and failure rates 3
Tom Hougaard estimates general trading failure rates at 70-80%, but says failure in the prop-account arena runs 98-99%, because prop firms' real business model is collecting monthly evaluation fees.
data_point🗣 Tom Hougaardconviction: highprop tradingprop firms
So what: Prop-firm revenue depends on trader failure; treat funded-account offers as a fee business, not a capital partnership.
“it's no secret that trading is tough and there's a failure rate of 70 to 80 %, but in the prop arena that failure rate is up at 98 to 99 %” ▶ 6:18
Tom Hougaard argues that many trading podcasters act as customer-acquisition funnels for prop brokers, and he refuses to appear on shows tied to that agenda.
contrarian🗣 Tom Hougaardconviction: highprop brokerspodcasts
So what: Discount trading-media content that monetizes through prop-firm referrals; sponsor incentives shape the narrative that trading is easy.
“many of these podcasters, they're simply just funnels for prop brokers” ▶ 6:31
Tom Hougaard believes a young trader is better off building their own capital and learning with it than using prop accounts, which he says bleed traders dry under unfair trading conditions, even in the more regulated US setting.
thesis🗣 Tom Hougaardconviction: highprop tradingUSEurope
So what: Building your own capital avoids fee drag and conditions stacked against you; this reframes the 'capital is the constraint' pitch.
Small-cap shorting: cut out whole categories 7
David Capablanca removed entire categories of small caps from his universe (nano-float stocks and Chinese-domiciled stocks) after finding they are the common denominator of the outlier squeezes that end short sellers' careers.
framework🗣 David Capablancaconviction: highsmall capsnano floatChinese stocks
So what: Excluding a whole category cuts off the tail risk of a career-ending squeeze at the cost of a few trades, which is a better trade-off than sizing those names smaller.
David Capablanca says the recent small-cap outlier squeezes cluster in nano-float names, citing floats around 1.6 million shares over the past couple of years.
data_point🗣 David Capablancaconviction: mediumnano floatsmall caps
So what: Screen float size before shorting: sub-~2M floats carry outsized squeeze risk.
“Oh, it's the nano floats. Oh, it's actually 1.6 million float uh recently past couple years.” ▶ 38:12
David Capablanca says Chinese small caps ended many trading careers from 2023 to 2026, and he now blocks them out entirely, neither shorting nor going long, because the risk/reward is structurally bad.
thesis🗣 David Capablancaconviction: highChinese stockssmall caps
So what: Tools that flag where a stock is domiciled (he mentions AI and short-finder tools) let traders filter these names out automatically.
“So I don't even look at the Chinese anymore. I block them out. I can't even I can't even see them. It's like they don't exist.”
David Capablanca avoids even going long Chinese small caps because of indefinite T12 trading halts: he once had $50,000 locked in a short on a halted Chinese stock for 10 months.
anecdote🗣 David Capablancaconviction: highChinese stocksT12 halt
So what: Halt risk traps capital and keeps borrow fees running; liquidity risk applies to longs as well as shorts.
“I shorted one Chinese stock 2022. It was [snorts] it was T 12 halted for 10 months. I had I had $50,000 locked up for 10 months” ▶ 42:22
A hedge fund stuck short INHD during a halt or lawsuit told David Capablanca it faced about $1 million in borrow fees in a single month.
data_point🗣 David Capablancaconviction: mediumINHDhedge fundborrow fees
So what: Borrow costs during a halt can compound into losses large enough to sink a fund, so model halt duration times borrow rate before shorting illiquid names.
“he said I'm going to owe a million dollars in borrow fees this month” ▶ 1:54
Chinese small caps keep producing extreme moves, such as one squeezing from $1 to $37 in the pre-market and WFF going from $1 to $50 in a day, and David Capablanca treats each of these as a reason to stay away, not an opportunity.
data_point🗣 David Capablancaconviction: mediumChinese stocksWFF
So what: Extreme pre-market squeezes in foreign-domiciled micro caps are a hazard to shorts; avoid the setup no matter how stretched it looks.
David Capablanca argues that trading dangerous squeeze-prone names carries an opportunity cost, because it pulls attention away from easier, criteria-fitting shorts such as NCPL, which he shorted from his phone during the conference.
framework🗣 David Capablancaconviction: mediumNCPLsmall caps
So what: Narrowing the universe improves capital and attention allocation toward high-quality setups.
“you're missing opportunities, opportunity costs of the easy stock” ▶ 39:03
Trade busts and exchange tail risk 3
Exchange trade busts can retroactively wipe out gains: on BYA, trades in roughly the $3.05-and-up range over 4-5 minutes were cancelled, and one trader who kept trading on those phantom profits ended the day deeply down.
anecdote🗣 host (Kevin) / David Capablancaconviction: mediumBYANasdaqFINRAtrade bust
So what: Don't treat unsettled windfall profits in halted or erratic names as real capital until clearly erroneous trade reviews are resolved.
David Capablanca once lost $25,000 shorting a stock after an erroneous exchange reverse split, and only got the money back when the trade was busted.
anecdote🗣 David Capablancaconviction: lowreverse splittrade bust
So what: Corporate-action errors around reverse splits are a real operational risk for pre-market shorts.
The host cites a small-cap trader with a $30,000 account who ended up owing his broker millions of dollars, an example of the unlimited-loss tail in small-cap shorting.
data_point🗣 host (Kevin)conviction: mediumsmall capsshort selling
So what: On short positions in squeeze-prone names, account size does not cap the loss, so hard exclusion rules matter more than stop placement.
Adding to winners and trend days 6
Tom Hougaard says most of his money has been made when the market trends and he adds to winning positions, a practice he learned from mentor David Paul.
thesis🗣 Tom Hougaardconviction: highDavid Paultrend trading
So what: Expectancy comes from pyramiding into trends, not from a high hit rate.
“the vast majority of my money has been made on A, the market trending, and B, me adding adding to my positions in a trending market”
Tom Hougaard frames the core asymmetry this way: losers add to their losing trades and winners add to their winning trades.
framework🗣 Tom Hougaardconviction: highDavid Paul
So what: Audit your own adds: averaging down is a warning sign, scaling into profit is the edge.
“Losers add to their losing trades and winners add to their winning trades.” ▶ 48:39
From almost 10 years watching others on a trading floor, Tom Hougaard concluded that many traders are right 60-70% of the time but blow up on one or two trades because they cannot take a loss.
data_point🗣 Tom Hougaardconviction: highJ. P. Morgantrading floor
So what: A high win rate means little without loss control, so judge traders and strategies by their worst losses.
Tom Hougaard describes trend days, when the open is the high and the close is the low or the reverse, as the source of outsized returns; during the COVID crash he shorted all day and kept adding on such days.
anecdote🗣 Tom Hougaardconviction: highCOVID crashDowtrend day
So what: Recognizing a trend day early and pyramiding into it can produce a year's P&L in a session.
Tom Hougaard says that after making more on one trend day than in the previous two years combined, a trader is 'forever damaged' and can't go back to fixed one-to-one targets.
causal🗣 Tom Hougaardconviction: mediumtrend day
So what: Know your temperament: target-based and trend-adding styles are psychologically incompatible.
“you are forever damaged” ▶ 1:02:57
David Paul's guideline, as Tom Hougaard relates it, is that a position should be two to three times bigger at the finish than at the start; Hougaard misheard it as 20 to 30 times and traded that way.
framework🗣 Tom Hougaardconviction: mediumDavid Paul
So what: A concrete pyramiding rule: scale total size as the trend confirms.
Targets, exits and pain avoidance 5
Tom Hougaard rejects fixed profit targets because exiting at 40 and watching the market run to 60 hurts him more than taking a smaller exit at 30 after a pullback. He accepts giving back open profit in exchange for the chance of a much larger one.
contrarian🗣 Tom Hougaardconviction: highprofit targetsrisk reward
So what: Trailing or discretionary exits suit trend capture; fixed R-multiples cap the right tail.
Tom Hougaard says indicators like stochastics can give traders a subconscious excuse to exit profitable positions early, relieving the pain of holding open profit, and after one such exit the market kept running all day.
causal🗣 Tom Hougaardconviction: mediumstochasticsBollinger BandFibonacciGann
So what: Audit indicator-driven exits for whether the signal is really an emotional escape hatch.
“the stochastics actually stops you from seeing the market for what it is” ▶ 0:39
David Paul's principle, as Tom Hougaard relays it: a single trade's outcome is random, and a statistical edge only shows up over 10 to 100 trades, yet most traders quit after about three straight losses when the amygdala takes over.
framework🗣 Tom Hougaardconviction: highDavid Paulamygdala
So what: Judge a strategy on samples of 100 trades, not short streaks; pre-commit to taking the signals.
“on the scale of one trade the outcome is completely random. But on the scale of 10 trades that's where statistics kick in” ▶ 32:52
David Capablanca says he no longer uses risk/reward ratios to pick small-cap shorts; his system's exclusions (for example Chinese stocks) screen out bad risk/reward automatically, which he likens to Wall Street desks trading their given criteria.
framework🗣 David Capablancaconviction: mediumrisk rewardsmall caps
So what: Rules about what not to trade can replace per-trade R:R math.
As a short seller, David Capablanca uses a long-term thesis about where a pumped small cap 'belongs' to select it, then waits for extension and targets a give-back of most of that extension, re-entering because these stocks 'have nine lives'.
framework🗣 David Capablancaconviction: mediumsmall capsshort selling
So what: Retail traders' edge over institutions is agility: they can re-short repeated pump cycles in the same ticker.
“these stocks they have like nine lives. They just like you know they they drop, they come back.” ▶ 54:34
Position sizing, scaling and seasonality 6
David Capablanca grades setups A through C. A scalp (B or B-minus) gets half size, split into four 'bullets', while A-plus setups, such as GLD, get full size scaled in across four or five bullets.
framework🗣 David Capablancaconviction: mediumGLD
So what: Grade setups by quality and size each grade differently, entering in tranches instead of one block.
David Capablanca changes his account size by season: about $50,000-$100,000 in summer (wiring out profits) and $200,000-$500,000 in stronger periods, with a maximum A-setup position anywhere from $50,000 to a quarter million.
data_point🗣 David Capablancaconviction: mediumsmall caps
So what: Sizing capital at risk to the opportunity season improves returns without changing the strategy itself.
David Capablanca expects a seasonal Thanksgiving and holiday pump-and-dump cycle in small caps, as promoters spread tips during family gatherings, and treats it as an A-plus shorting season.
prediction🗣 David Capablancaconviction: mediumpump and dumpsThanksgivingBlack FridaySanta Claus run
So what: Watch for holiday-week promotional spikes in small caps as short opportunities.
“there's a season to pump and dumps” ▶ 1:12:11
David Capablanca says he sizes much smaller away from his desk: he shorted NCPL with 2,500 then 5,000 shares from his phone but would have used 10,000 or more on his full setup, and he only does this for stocks he has already researched and 'stalked'.
anecdote🗣 David Capablancaconviction: mediumNCPL
So what: Mobile or distracted execution should mean smaller size and only on pre-researched names.
Tom Hougaard says scaling up is the same process at larger size and must be built through mental stamina. He cites pit trader 'Rambo' (John Wold Dean), who was 'an awful 20 lot trader', then 40, then 80, until each size became normal.
framework🗣 Tom Hougaardconviction: highRamboJohn Wold Dean
So what: Grow size step by step and accept a period of discomfort at each new level.
“winning is easy. The real challenge is losing” ▶ 51:45
Tom Hougaard says that for already-profitable traders, the next level comes neither from error-free execution nor from scaling-in technique, but from never settling, such as refusing to stay at 15 lots once 15 feels comfortable.
contrarian🗣 Tom Hougaardconviction: medium
So what: Above breakeven, the binding constraint is psychological appetite for size, not technique.
“it's not about scaling in. It's not about being right or wrong. It's about not being satisfied with where you are right now” ▶ 0:55

In Good Company · Nicolai Tangen 1 episode(s) · 30 insights

Ruchir Sharma: Why the World Is Betting on America | Podcast | In Good Company32 min30 insights2026-11-27
▶ Watch on YouTube
Ruchir Sharma argues the AI bubble is deflating slowly as rising rates, Big Tech's shift to debt financing and 6.5%-of-GDP US deficits end the easy-money regime, while 80% of global equity flows chase the US even as US stocks stopped outperforming. He sees China as a deflationary drag, EM as an extension of the AI trade except India (the anti-AI trade), and favors a basket of ~250 AI-agnostic global quality stocks for the next 3-5 years.
AI bubble: four O's and the rate trigger · 8Deficits and the end of easy money · 5America's capital magnet · 6China's drag and EM misnomer · 52027 outlook and the quality basket · 3Investor temperament and mob lessons · 3
AI bubble: four O's and the rate trigger 8
Ruchir Sharma says bubbles typically deflate over two to three years rather than at a single moment, so there is no clean point at which the AI bubble can be declared burst.
framework🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: highAI
So what: Expect a drawn-out topping process; cracks accumulate over months before a recognized turning point.
Sharma's 'four O's' framework for identifying bubbles is overvaluation, over-ownership, over-leverage and over-trading, and he judged the AI trade to be at an advanced bubble stage on all four.
framework🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: highAI
So what: Use the four O's as a checklist for late-cycle risk in AI-linked equities.
Sharma argues the only factor that deflates a bubble is a rise in the price of money, and rising rates are now making AI financing much more difficult.
causal🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: highAIinterest rates
So what: Watch long-end yields and credit costs as the key trigger for AI capex retrenchment.
“There's only one factor which deflates a bubble when the price of money goes up.” ▶ 2:01
Sharma predicts that over the coming months AI financing will get harder and big projected capex numbers will start coming down, unless a visible productivity boost arrives almost immediately and pulls rates lower.
prediction🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: mediumAICapEx
So what: Downward revisions to hyperscaler capex guidance would hit AI hardware and infrastructure suppliers.
Big Tech has shifted from funding AI from its own cash flow to raising debt and becoming net-debt companies, making the boom dependent on external financing.
causal🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: highBig TechAI
So what: Credit markets, not just equity sentiment, now determine the pace of AI spending; monitor tech bond issuance and spreads.
Sharma says the AI ecosystem has already borrowed about $600 billion this year, competing for capital alongside a US federal deficit of more than $2 trillion, unlike 2000 when the government ran a surplus.
data_point🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: highAIUS government
So what: Combined private AI borrowing and public deficits put structural upward pressure on interest rates.
“the amount of borrowing that the AI ecosystem has done is already 600 billion” ▶ 4:22
Sharma reads the sharp increase in Nasdaq and broader market volatility as a technical sign that characterizes a late-stage bubble cycle.
framework🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: mediumNasdaq
So what: Rising volatility alongside elevated valuations is a signal to reduce concentrated AI exposure.
Sharma frames AI as a fantastic technological revolution that has nonetheless turned into mob behavior, with non-AI sectors seeing flat to negative investment growth as capital is sucked into one industry.
contrarian🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: highAI
So what: An economy where non-AI sectors shrink cannot keep buying AI output, undermining the boom's demand base.
“there are instances in history when the wisdom turns into a mob” ▶ 23:09
Deficits and the end of easy money 5
Sharma says the US is running a budget deficit of about 6.5% of GDP, which is unprecedented outside a crisis and is drawing capital away from other markets.
data_point🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: highUS government
So what: Persistent large deficits support a higher term premium on long-dated Treasuries.
Sharma attributes a healthy chunk of the rise in interest rates from sub-1% five years ago to around 5% to persistently large government deficits, marking a regime shift away from easy money.
thesis🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: highinterest ratesUS Treasuries
So what: Position for structurally higher rates rather than a return to the zero-rate era.
“the regime of very easy money that we have is over” ▶ 5:52
Sharma expects US government spending as a share of GDP to keep rising because gridlock between a Democratic-controlled Congress and President Trump makes higher spending the easiest compromise; the deficit dipped into the fives in 2025 but is back in the sixes.
prediction🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: mediumUS CongressDonald Trump
So what: Fiscal consolidation is unlikely without market pressure, keeping supply of Treasuries heavy.
Sharma argues policymakers have zero incentive to act until a crisis, so government spending will keep rising until bond markets hit a 'riot point'; for now the bond market is only protesting.
prediction🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: mediumbond market
So what: Expect episodic bond selloffs; a disorderly long-end move is the tail risk to watch.
“The bond market isn't quite revolting, but it is at least beginning to protest.” ▶ 13:40
Long-end rates have risen globally, with spreads widening most for countries running larger deficits; France is exhibit A, with government spending as a share of GDP second only to North Korea, per Sharma.
data_point🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: mediumFranceBritainJapanNorth Korea
So what: Favor sovereigns with better fiscal positions; French spreads are a gauge of fiscal stress in Europe.
America's capital magnet 6
Sharma says roughly 80% of global equity flows over the past year went into the US, versus a historical average of about 40%, while the US is only about 28% of the global economy.
data_point🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: highAmerica
So what: Extreme flow concentration in US equities raises vulnerability to any reversal.
“80% of the flows have gone into, equity flows have gone into one country, which is America. This has never happened before.” ▶ 10:48
Foreign ownership of the US stock market has risen to nearly 25%, from below 10% a couple of decades ago, driven by retail as well as institutional investors.
data_point🗣 Ruchir Sharmaconviction: highAmerica
So what: A large foreign holder base makes US equities and the dollar sensitive to shifts in foreign sentiment.
