This Week in Startups · Jason Calacanis 1 episode(s) · 29 insights

30 Pilots, Zero Customers: Why VCs Keep Passing | Ask Jason61 min29 insights2026-11-25
▶ Watch on YouTube
An Ask Jason Q&A where Jason Calacanis and Lon argue that one paying, delighted (ideally 'lighthouse') customer beats any number of unpaid pilots, that founder obsession and persistence is the real moat against commoditization by Anthropic/OpenAI, and that Austin's cost structure favors second-time founders while San Francisco density favors first-timers. It closes with a pitch from List to Bid (listed bit), a marketplace where agents bid on commissions, with an end goal of AI-driven, realtor-free home sales; roughly a third of the runtime is sponsor reads.
Traction: paid customers over pilots · 5Reading VC rejections and founder fit · 5Moats in the AI commoditization era · 5AI workflows for operators · 2Startup geography: Austin vs Bay Area · 3Space careers and orbital data centers · 2List to Bid and real estate commission disruption · 7
Traction: paid customers over pilots 5
Jason Calacanis argues that 30 unpaid pilots signal a founder is not hardcore; VCs want to see one delighted, paying customer, and a founder with 30 pilots should cut to three paid ones.
contrarian🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: highSwipe Health
So what: When diligencing early-stage deals, discount unpaid pilots/LOIs/MOUs heavily and look for conversion to paid usage.
“if you have 30 pilots why can't you have three paid customers? Why don't you fire 27 pilots and just get to three paid ones?” ▶ 44:10
Calacanis says pilots, letters of intent and memorandums of understanding are weak, non-binding signals that investors read as a lack of commitment.
thesis🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: high
So what: Non-binding commercial commitments should not be treated as revenue-quality traction in valuations.
“The point isn't pilots. Pilots are for cowards.” ▶ 44:19
Calacanis says a single customer quote showing expansion from $100 a month to $1,100 a month is more powerful to VCs than startup competition awards, incubator stints, or advisor lists.
framework🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: highTech Stars
So what: Net revenue expansion from even one account is a stronger early signal than credentials; screen pitches for it.
“VCs will want to see just one customer who's over the moon, who's using your product and paying you.” ▶ 41:29
A founder whose company has existed two years, gone through three incubators and won many startup tournaments signals to VCs that the idea is weak and the founder is focused on the wrong metrics.
framework🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: high
So what: Serial accelerator participation without revenue is a red flag in early-stage screening.
Lon describes the 'lighthouse customer' framework: one respected, named customer (for example a frontier AI lab) is enough for a winning pitch, because other buyers follow it; a launch accelerator company won first place weekly on the strength of having one frontier lab as a client.
framework🗣 Lonconviction: highopen AIanthropicGrock
So what: For AI infrastructure/tooling startups, a frontier-lab customer is a powerful validation signal that can dominate VC perception.
“if an open AI if an anthropic level company is working with this provider those people have their pick of any AI partner they want in the world this must be a worthwhile company” ▶ 46:35
Reading VC rejections and founder fit 5
Calacanis says 'we need to see more traction' is often a polite excuse from investors who will never invest; founders should explicitly ask for the three biggest blockers and brutal honesty, and roughly one in three investors will actually give it.
framework🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: high
So what: Stated VC pass reasons are low-information; the real reasons (low margins, hard go-to-market, also-ran product) must be extracted directly.
“Most investors will just snow you.” ▶ 42:18
Some investors will back an exceptional team (e.g. out of Stanford) before a product launches, so a lack of interest after launch may mean the product is seen as an also-ran, too hard to bring to market, or low-margin.
causal🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: mediumStanford
So what: Funding difficulty post-launch is itself a market signal about product quality or unit economics.
Calacanis says many founders are 'tourists in startup founderland' or performative, and anyone unwilling to cancel everything in their life to get that first customer should not be a founder.
thesis🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: high
So what: Founder commitment is a primary screening factor in Calacanis's early-stage investing.
“If you're not willing to cancel everything in your life to make your startup work and get that one customer, don't be a founder.” ▶ 43:04
An early-stage elevator pitch should cover in under 60 seconds who the customer is, their problem, the solution, and how the company makes money; inability to do so suggests a poorly constructed project, while late-stage investors instead want growth, margins and two years of charts.
framework🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: highUberRobin Hood
So what: A usable screening heuristic: founders who cannot crisply articulate customer, problem and monetization are higher risk.
“If you can't do something like that, it's probably because you do not have a well- constructed project.” ▶ 2:58
Calacanis frames the Robin Hood thesis as onboarding a generation with free, fractional trading monetized via payment for order flow, then growing with them into 529s, 401ks, IRAs and margin loans over a decade.
framework🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: mediumRobin Hood
So what: Customer-lifetime expansion from a free entry product is the template investors look for in consumer fintech such as new trading apps.
“we're going to be the onboarding for that generation” ▶ 3:39
Moats in the AI commoditization era 5
Calacanis argues that because anyone can now build anything with AI, the real moat for an AI startup is a founder and team so obsessed with a problem, product or customer base that they keep iterating for years without quitting.
thesis🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: highanthropicopen AI
So what: In AI app-layer investing, weight founder persistence and customer love over technical defensibility, which is eroding.
“staying in the game and being relentless and continuing to build it and surviving is the magic.” ▶ 24:28
Calacanis says Canva is currently facing commoditization from Claude design and other tools, and Figma faces the same pressure, but both can endure if they keep shipping great products because customers pay little relative to the value received.
thesis🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: mediumCanvaFigmaClaude
So what: Design-software incumbents face real AI-native competitive pressure; watch pricing power and customer retention as the tell.
“Canva is going through that right now with claw design and many other things.” ▶ 24:46
Calacanis notes that competitors who said annotation was 'already done' when he bought annotated.com 15 years ago have all quit and their products are defunct, illustrating that survival itself is the moat.
anecdote🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: mediumannotated.com
So what: Crowded early markets often clear out through attrition; persistent players can inherit them.
A founder who suddenly takes on side quests such as conferences, starting a venture fund or a podcast may not be truly obsessed; only very successful founders like Elon can run side quests because they have unlimited talent and resources while staying focused on the core.
framework🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: mediumElon
So what: Founder distraction is a measurable risk factor that could be built into startup scoring models.
Calacanis advises a small investment firm using a 'FICO score for startups' to concentrate on the three founders it can help most and be ride-or-die with them rather than spread thin.
framework🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: medium
So what: Concentrated, high-touch support may beat broad portfolio coverage for small early-stage investors.
AI workflows for operators 2
Calacanis uses his Grockbot to triage Slack, email and DMs into multiple-choice decisions and asks it where he is a blocker for others, which also gives the AI full context of his life.
anecdote🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: mediumGrockbotSlack
So what: Executive-assistant style agent workflows are a live use case among power users, a signal for agentic productivity tools.
Lon says cross-checking outputs between models, such as pasting Grockbot results into Claude and vice versa, roughly doubled the quality of their Substack posts, and pressing an LLM to 'unpack' claims often surfaces that it invented a connection.
data_point🗣 Lonconviction: mediumGrockbotClaudechat GPT
So what: Multi-model usage by power users argues against single-vendor lock-in for consumer LLM products.
“the substacks got so much better when I started copying pasting Grockbot results into Claude and vice versa.” ▶ 13:37
Startup geography: Austin vs Bay Area 3
Calacanis cites one-bedroom rent of $4,000 to $6,000 in the San Francisco Bay Area versus roughly $800 to $1,200 within 20 miles of central Austin, and says a family home in the Bay Area costs a minimum of $5 million.
data_point🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: highSan FranciscoAustin
So what: Cost-of-living gaps drive senior talent and second-time founders toward Austin, supporting Texas startup ecosystem growth.
“Starting a family and having a home in the Bay Area is a minimum of a $5 million per home.” ▶ 35:00
Calacanis estimates a $5 million Bay Area home at 7% implies about $35,000 a month in payments, requiring $75,000 to $100,000 a month of household income, which is why Bay Area salaries reach $500k to $1.5 million.
causal🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: highSan Francisco
So what: Bay Area housing costs structurally inflate startup compensation and burn rates.
Calacanis recommends first-time founders go to San Francisco, then New York, then Austin for density, while funded second-time founders should HQ in Austin, where a three-bedroom home costs $250k to $1 million and taxes are about 15% lower.
framework🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: highSan FranciscoAustinY Combinator
So what: Founder stage predicts optimal location; experienced Austin-based teams may run leaner burn.
“I'd say first-time founders, you're in the mix. Yeah. Go to San Francisco immediately.” ▶ 36:32
Space careers and orbital data centers 2
Lon says orbital data centers are the most interesting space opportunity right now because terrestrial data centers face growing local opposition, noting Google is already launching its own and StarCloud is working on it.
thesis🗣 Lonconviction: mediumGoogleStarCloud
So what: Local backlash against AI data centers could accelerate investment in space-based compute; watch Google and StarCloud progress.
“now that we know how much people hate them here on Earth, I feel like there's so much push like how do we figure this out ASAP and Google's already launching theirs” ▶ 31:33
Calacanis advises students targeting the space industry to build a public brand around space (Substack, podcast, social media) and join very early-stage space startups, where founders hand real work to anyone who shows initiative.
framework🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: mediumY CombinatorTech Stars
So what: Early-stage space startups are talent-constrained; hiring access is a bottleneck for the sector.
List to Bid and real estate commission disruption 7
List to Bid founder Cam says sellers post a home in seconds and agents bid on commission; a featured agent with 33 years' experience sold a $750,000 home at 1% commission versus the typical 6%, saving about $30,000.
data_point🗣 Cam Sapanlooconviction: highList to Bid
So what: Commission compression marketplaces target the 6% norm that, per the founder, recent regulatory action failed to move.
Cam says the platform has helped about 550 additional sellers across the US for a combined $500 million in real estate volume, with agents from top brokerages including Compass and Kelly Williams (as transcribed).
data_point🗣 Cam Sapanlooconviction: highCompassKelly Williams
So what: Early traction metric for a proptech marketplace challenging traditional brokerage economics.
“We've helped another an additional 550 sellers across the US for a combined value of $500 million in real estate volume.” ▶ 51:52
Cam says that despite recent industry action against the brokerage model to lower commissions, fees in practice remain exactly as before, because commissions have not moved for decades while home prices have.
causal🗣 Cam Sapanlooconviction: high
So what: Regulation alone has not compressed real estate commissions, leaving room for market-based disruptors; brokerage take rates remain at risk.
“For decades, commissions haven't moved. Home prices have.” ▶ 52:58
List to Bid monetizes through agent monthly subscriptions to bid plus a 7.5% to 10% split of the winning agent's commission, and its end goal is an AI-driven marketplace with no realtors, escrow, title or fees, two-day escrow, and closing costs cut from about 9% to roughly $500.
prediction🗣 Cam Sapanlooconviction: highList to Bid
So what: If transaction data enables AI agents to replace realtors, escrow and title, incumbent brokerages and title/escrow businesses face disintermediation.
“The end goal for listed is we want no realtors, no escrow, no title, no fees.” ▶ 52:25
Cam says about 20% of realtors take about 80% of market share and that 74% of realtors had no transaction last year; after repositioning bids as 'proposals', 90%+ of agents approached now join because the platform controls the listings.
data_point🗣 Cam Sapanlooconviction: high
So what: Listing control gives marketplaces leverage over a highly concentrated, oversupplied agent base.
“I think the statistic was last year 74% of all realtors didn't have a single transaction.” ▶ 55:20
Cam says investors' main objection to proptech is battle scars from a highly regulated, slow-moving industry, which he counters by planning to obtain a brokerage license so agents work under the company and build a brokerage moat.
thesis🗣 Cam Sapanlooconviction: medium
So what: Regulatory friction remains the key investor risk in proptech; vertical integration into brokerage is one mitigation path.
Calacanis says advisor equity grants (25 to 50 bips, up to 1%) should vest over time like an employee's, carry a defined scope of work, and be cancellable by either party, because some advisors receive equity and do no work.
framework🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: highPhil Helmuth
So what: Founders and investors should check cap tables for unvested or unscoped advisor grants.

Risk Reversal · Dan Nathan 1 episode(s) · 29 insights

The Stock Market Is Doing the Fed’s Job48 min29 insights2026-11-25
▶ Watch on YouTube
Ben Evans of Fed Watch Advisors argues Nvidia's selloff alone equated to ~1.5 Fed hikes of tightening, so the stock market is doing the Fed's job: he sees a 25bp December hike as locked in but a January skip likely. He flags AI credit stress in neoclouds like CoreWeave (GPU prices vs token prices as the trigger), doubts consumer AI monetization, and prefers IBM, AMD, Google and Microsoft over edge plays like SanDisk and CoreWeave.

Stock market as Fed tightening proxy 5
Ben Evans calculated, using the Fed's financial-conditions framework (a percentage change in equities equated to 25bp of hikes/cuts), that Nvidia's stock decline alone over the past week equated to roughly one and a half Fed rate hikes of tightening.
data_point🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: highNvidiaFed
So what: Nvidia's index weight makes its drawdowns a macro tightening event; watch NVDA volatility as a real-time gauge of financial conditions.
“I calculated that the Nvidia move alone just the stock alone moving down accounted almost for one and a half rate hikes by the Fed” ▶ 5:47
Because Nvidia sits in every ETF and carries large index weight, its volatility is now systemic: sharp NVDA drawdowns signal the stock market is pricing in Fed tightening on the Fed's behalf.
causal🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: highNvidiaFed
So what: Treat NVDA drawdowns as de facto policy tightening that may reduce the need for further Fed hikes.
“the stock market is doing it for the fed” ▶ 7:51
The US economy has shifted from a credit/consumption economy to an AI-driven investment economy, which brings higher growth and productivity, therefore structurally higher rates, more speculation, and more volatility.
thesis🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: highAI
So what: Position for a higher-rate, higher-volatility regime rather than a return to low-rate consumption-led growth.
“We're now I think really in an investment economy as opposed to years ago more of a credit consumption economy” ▶ 5:04
A 20-30% equity decline puts the AI investment cycle itself in doubt and hits consumer spending via the wealth effect, so the stock market is effectively driving the economy right now.
causal🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumAIS&P
So what: Equity drawdowns feed back into capex and consumption data with a lag; expect macro data to soften after market shocks.
The economy is so financialized that policymakers (the administration and possibly the Fed) will not let an AI-led selloff 'run into an abyss'; the president's intervention to bring Jensen Huang back was a signal the administration does not want the capex cycle derailed.
thesis🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumJensenNvidiaAI capex
So what: Implies a policy put under the AI complex; sharp selloffs may be met with intervention.
“we can't let it run into an abyss because the economy is too financialized” ▶ 14:49
Fed path: hike locked, then pause 6
Fed officials (over 22 speakers in a week, with Waller notable) have been hawkish, messaging that inflation is too far above target and risks de-anchoring toward 3%, while deliberately letting markets do part of the tightening rather than slamming the brakes with something like a 100bp hike.
thesis🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: highFedWaller
So what: Fed communication is itself a tightening tool; hawkish Fed-speak plus market drawdowns substitute for larger hikes.
“let signal to the markets this is our clear intent and let the markets do part of our job” ▶ 9:53
Ben Evans views a 25bp Fed hike at the December 9 FOMC meeting as locked in given sticky inflation, and floated 50bp for debate, noting the minutes showed some members leaning that way if inflation does not improve.
prediction🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: highFedFOMC
So what: Rate-sensitive positioning should assume a December hike; upside risk of 50bp if PCE/inflation data disappoint.
“So this rate hike is locked in” ▶ 42:56
Ben Evans's updated call is that market-driven tightening has been so large (multiple hikes' worth) that the Fed can hike in December and then skip January, moving one step at a time as it did when it skipped October.
prediction🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumFed
So what: Look for a pause in January; front-end rates may overprice a staircase of consecutive hikes.
“I think they'll skip January” ▶ 47:13
If the Fed does not hike in December, stocks would get short-term relief but long yields would pop, and markets would re-price tightening if inflation does not improve; Ben Evans has a 6% 10-year yield call, which the host called aggressive.
prediction🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumFed10-year yield
So what: A dovish surprise is a bear-steepener risk; any equity relief rally may be short-lived.
“We go higher with the 10ear yield. You know, my call right at 6%.” ▶ 46:19
A Q4 2018-style Fed pivot is unlikely because then inflation was below target and the escalating China trade war raised deflation risk; today inflation is above target and the bigger wild card is the war in Iran, not a China trade war.
contrarian🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: highFedChinaIran
So what: Don't bet on a Fed put via pivot after the 20% selloff analog; the macro setup differs.
“We're in a totally different situation today. We're above target.” ▶ 45:51
Upcoming ISM, PMI and construction data will likely still be strong but will begin to reflect the October-November market shock, while the unresolved Iran conflict (crude around $80, ships being attacked) remains a risk factor.
prediction🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumISMIrancrude oil
So what: Watch December macro prints for first signs of shock transmission into activity.
AI credit risk and neoclouds 7
Credit risk in AI sits not with the hyperscalers themselves but with less well-capitalized companies financed through them, a stress that started with Oracle, moved to CoreWeave, and then to names like AMD and Micron.
thesis🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumOracleCoreweaveAMDMicron
So what: Monitor credit spreads and single-name CDS of AI-levered borrowers as the stress transmission channel.
CoreWeave has a roughly $23 billion market cap against about $66 billion of debt, with some unsecured debt trading at junk levels, and neoclouds would be first to lose contracts if AI demand disappoints.
data_point🗣 Dan Nathan (host)conviction: highCoreweaveNvidia
So what: Neocloud equity and debt are the highest-beta, most fragile expression of AI demand risk.
“It's got a $23 billion market cap. It's got $66 billion in debt.” ▶ 21:55
Ben Evans calculates the internal rate of return on a GPU at over 22% because compute demand is enormous and there is no GPU glut yet; a GPU glut or falling GPU values would put CoreWeave in big trouble given its debt-heavy balance sheet backed mainly by Nvidia.
data_point🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumCoreweaveNvidiaGPUs
So what: Track GPU rental prices (and the forthcoming CME GPU derivatives) as the key leading indicator for neocloud solvency.
“the internal rate of return on the GPU is over 22%” ▶ 22:16
The key stress signal is the divergence between falling token prices and rising GPU rental prices; if GPU prices start falling sharply due to oversupply, the AI ecosystem comes under stress. CME is planning GPU derivatives that would let investors trade this.
framework🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumGPUsCMENvidia
So what: Watch token price vs GPU rental price spread; a GPU price break is the trigger for AI credit stress.
AI financing differs from pre-GFC SPVs: it appears to be largely conventional bond issuance rather than opaque AAA-labeled structured products, and credit markets show essentially no stress yet, though off-balance-sheet debt is opaque and should be watched.
contrarian🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumAI capexCDS
So what: Don't assume a GFC replay; but watch single-name CDS, credit-spread options and financing channels for building stress.
“there seems to be zero stress really building there yet” ▶ 19:16
Markets punish hyperscalers (e.g. Meta, Google) when they announce higher capex funded by borrowing, which Ben Evans interprets as the stock market pricing in credit risk.
causal🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumMetaGoogle
So what: Capex-guidance-up announcements are near-term negative catalysts for hyperscaler stocks.
Ben Evans's 'Genron' framing: the SEC is examining accounting tied to Nvidia's Singapore operation, the first critical look at how Nvidia derives GPU profits in competitive markets, though he does not believe Nvidia is inflating profits.
anecdote🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: exploratoryNvidiaSECSingapore

Jensen Huang's reported comment about an 'avalanche of wafers and memory' and Nvidia's push to sell into Asian markets via Singapore/Malaysia suggests supply may outrun US frontier-lab demand, raising risk of a GPU oversupply as Asian competition drives prices down.
thesis🗣 Dan Nathan (host)conviction: mediumNvidiaJensenAnthropicOpenAIChina
So what: Semis are cyclical; overbuilding of GPUs and data centers is likely and would compress margins.
“we have an avalanche of wafers and memory coming in” ▶ 25:59
Overbuilding in semis and data centers is inevitable in this cycle, and the AI valuation premium will deflate as growth rates normalize and competition rises; the recent selloff was the first sign of that reassessment.
prediction🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumNvidiasemiconductorsdata centers
So what: Expect multiple compression for AI leaders even if the capex cycle continues.
Nvidia's growth rates will by definition decline (host cites ~100% this year, ~70% expected next), though Jensen's strategy of selling into Asia within export-control boundaries keeps producing upside surprises.
prediction🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumNvidiaJensen
So what: Decelerating growth vs. continued beats is the core tension in NVDA's multiple.
AI monetization: consumer vs enterprise 4
AI subscriptions work like a newspaper model; the broad consumer will not pay for extra tokens for everyday tasks like trip planning and will revert to free Google search, limiting consumer AI monetization to niche power users.
thesis🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumClaudeAnthropicGoogle
So what: Consumer AI revenue assumptions may be too high; profit expectations tied to compute-shortage pricing will come under pressure.
Microsoft Office 365 (about 450 million users, ~20 million Copilot subscribers cited) and Google's free, search-subsidized suite are the templates OpenAI and Anthropic will likely converge on: broad use at low, discounted fees.
framework🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumMicrosoftGoogleOpenAIAnthropic
So what: Expect frontier-lab pricing to fall toward mass-market levels, pressuring per-user revenue.
Anthropic has traded poorly post-IPO because investors question how it will build a global subscription market and become the AI benchmark the way Microsoft became the software benchmark; investors are in 'show me' mode ahead of its first earnings call.
thesis🗣 Ben Evans (Fed Watch Advisors)conviction: mediumAnthropicMicrosoft
So what: Anthropic's first earnings call is a key catalyst for pure-play AI sentiment; OpenAI IPO may follow within months.
Meta stock rallied about 30% after introducing Muse as an implied monetization engine despite a capex raise from $135B to $140B, then gave it all back as investors questioned the actual monetization model.
anecdote🗣 Dan Nathan (host)conviction: mediumMetaMuse
So what: AI monetization narratives without disclosed economics are not sticky for stock prices.

Real Vision · Raoul Pal 1 episode(s) · 28 insights

Ledger’s Ian Rogers: AI Is Creating a Security Nightmare | at Breakpoint 2026 in London28 min28 insights2026-11-26
▶ Watch on YouTube
Ledger's Chief Human Agency Officer Ian Rogers argues AI agents create a security nightmare and positions Ledger as the hardware-backed approval and policy layer that keeps humans in the loop (Agent Intent, Ledger Enterprise HSM policy engine). He also covers the ~$6B Binance hack, Ledger's distributor supply-chain incident, the Android-based Ledger Atom with the Solana Seeker app store, and clear signing arriving on Solana via Jupiter.
AI agents as a security problem · 5Ledger agent approval products · 6Hacks and the security arms race · 6Ledger Atom and Solana Seeker · 4Clear signing comes to Solana · 4Crypto TAM and Solana positioning · 3
AI agents as a security problem 5
Ian Rogers argues AI agents are a 'security nightmare' because users now share secrets with agents and trust them to act, so secrets must be shared securely, be revocable, and be recoverable if the device or agent is lost.
thesis🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: highLedgerAI agents
So what: Key management, revocation and recovery for agent credentials is an emerging security market that hardware-security vendors are positioning to capture.
“what's coming with AI is is a security nightmare” ▶ 3:09
Ledger is repositioning itself as a cryptography and security company rather than a cryptocurrency application, using smart-card chips (as in credit cards and passports), a secure OS and secure hardware to keep humans in the loop on AI-agent actions.
thesis🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: highLedgersecure elementAI
So what: Signals Ledger's TAM expansion beyond crypto wallets into AI-agent authorization; watch for non-crypto enterprise and app partnerships.
“Ledger has fundamentally is not a cryptocurrency application. It is a cryptography and security application” ▶ 3:29
Rogers frames AI alignment as a top-three problem for decades and argues alignment in practice is only achievable if every real-world AI action results from either an explicit human approval or a human-set policy, which also creates an audit trail.
framework🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: highAI alignmentpolicy engines
So what: Regulated domains (finance, IT, healthcare) will need auditable approval/policy layers for agents; a product category for policy-engine and HSM vendors.
“the only way we get alignment is if actions that AI takes in the real world are the product of either an explicit human approval or a policy that was set by a human” ▶ 9:44
Rogers expects that organizations will not be able to blame 'the agent' for decisions; every agent decision will require an audit trail showing which policy permitted it.
prediction🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumAI agentsenterprise compliance
So what: Compliance and governance tooling for agentic AI is likely to become mandatory spend for enterprises deploying agents.
Rogers notes AI in auto mode is often a better decision-maker than humans, who tend to approve prompts reflexively ('yes yes yes'), while AI actually examines each question.
contrarian🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumAI agents
So what: Supports a shift from per-action human approvals toward policy-based autonomy, which raises the value of policy-setting infrastructure.
Ledger agent approval products 6
Ledger announced Agent Intent, a platform that lets AI agents keep working by queuing proposed actions for asynchronous approval by the human operator on a Ledger device, rather than blocking on each signature.
data_point🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: highLedgerAgent Intent
So what: Hardware wallets becoming the approval layer for agents could open a recurring, non-crypto use case for Ledger devices.
Ledger integrated hardware-wallet funding into Solana's pay.sh, an agent payment system for paying for any API with stablecoins and micropayments that originally assumed a software wallet.
data_point🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumSolanapay.shLedgerstablecoins
So what: Agent-to-API stablecoin micropayments on Solana are being built with security add-ons, a sign of agentic payments maturing on that chain.
As a proof of concept, Ledger hooked Agent Intent up to OpenCl and delegated all of OpenCl's approvals to the Ledger queue.
data_point🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumOpenClAgent IntentLedger
So what: Early integration with agent frameworks; watch whether popular agent tooling adopts hardware-backed approval queues.
Rogers says he runs about 25 agents at any one time and that a single funnel for all agent approvals improves both ease of use and security, because otherwise tasks sit unnoticed waiting on the human for hours.
anecdote🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumAI agents
So what: Power users' agent sprawl creates demand for unified approval/orchestration interfaces.
Ledger Enterprise is already an HSM-backed policy engine that lets an AI agent manage the treasury of a bank or digital-asset company without holding keys, acting autonomously only within policy; Ledger aims to extend this to other domains.
thesis🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: highLedger EnterpriseHSMAI agents
So what: Institutional crypto treasuries can already delegate to agents under hardware policy controls; extension to non-crypto domains is the growth vector.
“today you can allow an agent to control the treasury of a bank or a company that manages digital assets because the agent doesn't have the keys” ▶ 10:52
Rogers argues hardware-backed authentication such as passkeys via Ledger is a rare case where security and ease of use improve together, because institutions that can verify identity can drop friction like whitelists, 24-hour waits and email links, much of which he calls security theater.
causal🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumLedgerpasskeysKraken
So what: Exchanges and banks adopting hardware passkeys could reduce onboarding/withdrawal friction, a UX and cost advantage.
Hacks and the security arms race 6
The Binance hack on October 29 saw around $6 billion stolen, reportedly by North Korean hackers, which Rogers characterizes as a classic insider-infiltration attack rather than something new.
data_point🗣 Arthur Rosinski (host) and Ian Rogersconviction: mediumBinanceNorth Korea
So what: Centralized-exchange insider risk remains the dominant attack vector; supports the self-custody case and insider-threat security spending.
Ledger CTO Charles Guillaumet's starting premise is that 'nothing is secure': security is a moving target requiring continuous investment, which is why Ledger uses upgradable firmware and runs the Dungeon attack lab.
framework🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: highCharles GuillaumetLedgerDungeon
So what: Security vendors that continuously invest in offensive research are better placed as AI raises attack capability.
“security is not perfection. Security is a moving target” ▶ 12:35
Ledger's attack lab this year disclosed a vulnerability affecting 25% of all Android phones, showing its research reaches beyond crypto.
data_point🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumLedgerAndroid
So what: Ledger's security research credibility extends to mainstream mobile, supporting its push into general app security.
A recent Ledger security incident was a supply-chain attack through a single distributor in Southeast Asia, long-running and expensive, affecting a very small number of devices; Rogers says he has no concerns about buying a Ledger from other sources.
data_point🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumLedgersupply chain
So what: Reputational risk to Ledger appears localized; buyers should purchase via official channels.
Rogers argues self-custody's distributed model is very expensive to attack on a relative basis, since no single action can take down a centralized network.
causal🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumself-custody
So what: Structural argument for self-custody over exchange custody after large CEX hacks.
Ledger has improved tamper resistance from early devices to the Ledger Nano Gen 5 touchscreen device, and its newest device, Ledger Atom, introduces a new tamper-resistance approach described in its white paper.
data_point🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: lowLedger Nano Gen 5Ledger Atom
So what: Product roadmap continues to harden hardware; relevant for evaluating hardware-wallet competition.
Ledger Atom and Solana Seeker 4
Ledger Atom is a device built on the Android Open Source Project that runs any Android app plus Ledger apps, with a secure mode where inputs on the screen are invisible to Android, merging phone and hardware wallet into one device.
data_point🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: highLedger AtomAndroid
So what: A secure-element Android device could compete with crypto phones and broaden Ledger's addressable market beyond hardware-wallet buyers.
Ledger has partnered with Solana Seeker so the entire Seeker store of more than 1,500 apps comes to Ledger Atom, Seeker's first hardware partnership outside its own device.
data_point🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: highSolana SeekerLedger AtomSolana
So what: Expands distribution for Solana mobile dApps and strengthens Solana's consumer-mobile positioning.
Rogers argues agentic coding removes the excuse for apps not to support niche secure hardware: a bank could add the Ledger SDK by telling Claude or Codex to do it, making banking or healthcare apps meaningfully more secure.
causal🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumClaudeCodexLedgeragentic coding
So what: AI coding lowers integration costs for small-installed-base platforms, which could accelerate third-party adoption of Ledger Atom.
“Well, now if they just tell Claude or Codex to do it, it'll do it.” ▶ 19:44
Rogers credits Solana Seeker with proving that a good mobile dApp experience is possible, and says crypto UX remains a big challenge; he estimates he got 75-80% of the experience job done in five years as Ledger's Chief Experience Officer.
anecdote🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: lowSolana SeekerLedger
So what: Crypto UX still incomplete; consumer-focused ecosystems like Solana may capture mainstream users first.
Clear signing comes to Solana 4
Ledger is bringing clear signing to Solana starting with Jupiter, announcing roughly 18 of the top Solana ecosystem actions that can now be clear signed on a Ledger after about a year of dedicated developer work.
data_point🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: highLedgerSolanaJupiterSolana Foundation
So what: Removes blind-signing risk for heavy Solana DeFi users, a meaningful security upgrade for Jupiter flows.
Rogers calls blind signing a thorn in Ledger's side: perfect security at rest is undone when users sign unreadable transactions, effectively signing a blank check; Ledger's CTO concluded users should no longer be allowed to blind sign on Ledger.
thesis🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: highLedgerblind signingEthereumSolana
So what: Transaction-time security is the weak link in self-custody; expect hardware wallets to push ecosystems toward readable transactions.
“I am now signing a blank check.” ▶ 21:13
Rogers' security framework requires three things: a private key generated and stored in a secure element, cryptographic operations in the secure element, and user signing on a readable secure screen, and he claims Ledger is the only solution checking all three.
framework🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumLedgersecure element
So what: Useful checklist for assessing wallet security claims and Ledger's competitive moat.
Implementing clear signing on Ethereum was far more difficult than anticipated because every smart contract needed a human-readable visual representation.
data_point🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumEthereumLedger
So what: Clear-signing coverage is a slow, ecosystem-wide effort; coverage gaps remain a risk on new protocols.

thesis🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumRaulAI agentscrypto

“we may have underestimated the TAM for crypto because we were only counting humans” ▶ 25:55
Rogers describes Solana in one word as 'consumer' and says the Solana community is among the only groups in crypto thinking about what the consumer internet needs, likening Breakpoint to E3 in the 1990s.
thesis🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumSolanaBreakpoint
So what: Supports a thesis of Solana as the consumer-app chain.

prediction🗣 Ian Rogersconviction: mediumSolanamultichain

The Great Simplification · Nate Hagens 1 episode(s) · 25 insights

Emerging Science On Human Resilience in a Warming World with Yadvinder Malhi | TGS 24471 min25 insights2026-11-25
▶ Watch on YouTube
Oxford ecologist Yadvinder Malhi argues nature work should prioritize adaptation to an expected ~2.5°C world over carbon accounting, warning of the 'carbonization of ecology' and the failure risk of tree-planting versus natural regeneration. Largely an ecology/values conversation with limited direct market content; notable items are a forthcoming UN Nature Relationship Index and skepticism toward carbon-centric forest valuation.
Limits of carbon framing for forests · 5Forests as water and climate engines · 3Climate trajectory and species migration · 6How nature recovery actually works · 5Governance, metrics and coordination · 6
Limits of carbon framing for forests 5
Yadvinder Malhi estimates rainforests absorb around 15% of human greenhouse gas emissions, slowing climate change but not acting as a game changer because emissions swamp the biosphere's absorptive capacity.
data_point🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: highrainforestsAmazoncarbon sequestration
So what: Forest offsets cannot substitute for emissions cuts at scale; nature-based carbon claims should be sized against this ceiling.
“15% of our greenhouse gas emissions” ▶ 11:51
Malhi warns of a 'carbonization of ecology': when carbon accounting becomes the worldview rather than a funding tool, forests get reduced to an extension of capital accounting systems.
contrarian🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: highcarbon accountingcarbon markets
So what: Signals ecologist skepticism toward carbon-credit-centric valuation of forests, a reputational and methodological risk for carbon-market products.
“what I sometimes call the carbonization” ▶ 8:51
Malhi sees a moral hazard in forest carbon narratives: the implicit assumption that 'trees will save us' can divert pressure from the core problem of cutting greenhouse gas emissions.
causal🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: highgreenhouse gas emissionscarbon offsets
So what: Offset-heavy net-zero strategies face growing credibility scrutiny from the scientific community.
Nuance that ecologists attach to carbon as 'a tool, not a worldview' tends to be lost when the language migrates into finance markets and financial accounting.
framework🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: mediumfinance marketscarbon accounting
So what: Watch for methodology gaps between scientific intent and how nature/carbon metrics are packaged in financial products.
Malhi says the biggest role of restoring nature is maximizing adaptation to warming already underway, not carbon sequestration or carbon budgets.
thesis🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: highnature recoveryclimate adaptation
So what: Suggests capital for nature should be judged on resilience/adaptation outcomes, not just tons sequestered.
Forests as water and climate engines 3
In the Amazon, winds from the Atlantic produce three or four cycles of rain and moisture regeneration as forest roots recycle water to the atmosphere, so the rainforest largely builds its own rain.
causal🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: highAmazonwater cyclingbiotic pump
So what: Deforestation risks degrading regional rainfall, a downstream risk for agriculture and hydropower dependent on Amazon moisture.
Malhi notes the Amazon is still about 80% intact, a forest the size of a continent despite losses.
data_point🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: mediumAmazon
So what: Large remaining intact area frames the Amazon as a protection priority rather than a restoration case.
“it's still 80% there” ▶ 17:32
Eddy-covariance towers (sonic anemometers plus infrared CO2 sensors) measured Amazon carbon uptake from the mid-1990s, but Malhi found forestry plot data on tree growth and death more reliable than the tower technology.
anecdote🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: mediumsonic anemometerAmazonforest monitoring
So what: Relevant to measurement/MRV providers: ground-truth inventory data still matters versus sensor-based flux estimates.
Climate trajectory and species migration 6
Malhi's central expectation for global warming is around 2.5°C above pre-industrial, which he calls the optimistic end of realistic.
prediction🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: mediumglobal warmingpre-industrial
So what: A scientist's base case of ~2.5°C implies investors should plan for substantial physical-climate adaptation needs.
“optimistic end of realistic” ▶ 32:58
On the Amazon-to-Andes gradient in Peru, insects, birds and mammals are moving upslope with warming while trees migrate far more slowly, breaking long-standing animal-host tree relationships.
data_point🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: highPeruAndesAmazon
So what: Ecosystem composition is shifting faster than restoration baselines assume; static conservation targets may misprice outcomes.
Mountains act as climate refugia, as in the ice ages when the Amazon was five degrees cooler, but extreme warming can push species to summits where they become isolated islands facing extinction risk.
causal🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: mediumAndesrefugiapaleoclimate
So what: Mountain biodiversity is a leading indicator of extinction risk under severe warming.
England's Forestry Commission is considering tree varieties genetically from Southern Europe that will be better adapted when trees mature in a few decades, rather than British genetic varieties.
data_point🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: mediumForestry CommissionEnglandSouthern Europe
So what: Climate-anticipatory forestry creates demand shifts in seed/nursery supply chains toward warmer-adapted genetics.
Malhi argues conservation should relax ideas of nativeness and prioritize connected landscapes over fragmented protected areas so species can migrate in a changing climate.
contrarian🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: mediumprotected areasconnectivitynativeness
So what: Policy may drift toward corridor and connectivity-based land use, affecting land-use planning and conservation finance design.
Malhi says policy (e.g., the UK Environment Agency) is starting to grasp process-based, change-oriented conservation, but public perception lags, partly from denial; the UK had its driest summer in 200 years.
thesis🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: mediumUK Environment Agencydrought
So what: Gap between policy and public opinion may slow adoption of adaptive conservation measures.
“we had the driest summer in 200 years” ▶ 36:18
How nature recovery actually works 5
Natural forest regeneration often yields a richer, more resilient ecosystem than tree planting, which reports well ('a thousand trees planted') and may grow faster but is more likely to fail.
contrarian🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: hightree plantingnatural regeneration
So what: Tree-count-based restoration and offset projects carry higher failure/permanence risk than natural regeneration approaches.
“a thousand trees planted that day” ▶ 28:39
Restoring abandoned farms succeeds far more where rich animal communities exist, because seed-dispersing birds and mammals, soil-disturbing pigs and rodents spreading mycorrhizal fungi drive recovery.
causal🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: highbiodiversitymycorrhizal fungirestoration
So what: Restoration project quality depends on surrounding fauna, a useful diligence criterion for nature-based investments.
Some ecosystems are locked into alternative states needing heavy intervention: on Tetiaroa atoll, invasive rats and abandoned coconut plantations suppressed seabirds whose guano fertilizes native forest, so rat eradication and palm removal were needed.
anecdote🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: mediumTetiaroaMarlon BrandoTahitiinvasive rats
So what: Not all degraded land recovers passively; restoration cost estimates should distinguish locked-in states.
At Cocha Cashu in Manu National Park, which holds one-ninth of all bird species, research by John Terborgh showed monkeys dispersing seeds end up shaping the rainforest canopy.
data_point🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: mediumManu National ParkCocha CashuJohn TerborghPeru
So what: Fauna loss (e.g., hunting) can degrade forest structure over time, a hidden risk for forest carbon permanence.
Malhi's research finds rewilding or smallholder agriculture can rebuild ecosystem energy flows ('vibrancy') far faster than they rebuild biodiversity, suggesting energy and nutrient flow metrics as an additional ecosystem health lens.
framework🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: exploratoryrewildingsmallholder agricultureenergy flow
So what: Possible emerging nature metric beyond species counts that could inform biodiversity-credit and nature-reporting methodologies.
“and rebuild those energy flows far faster than” ▶ 1:00:41
Governance, metrics and coordination 6
The Convention on Biological Diversity target of 30% of terrestrial and marine areas protected by 2030 is being met to varying degrees, and Malhi argues outcomes would be worse without it.
data_point🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: mediumConvention on Biological Diversity30 by 30
So what: 30x30 continues to drive protected-area expansion and national policy alignment, affecting land-use and extractive project siting.
Malhi's team is working with the UN Human Development Report to launch a Nature Relationship Index measuring protected areas, urban green space, biodiversity consumption patterns and footprint, with a first version due in the December report.
prediction🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: highUN Human Development ReportHuman Development IndexNature Relationship Index
So what: A new UN country-level nature metric could feed into sovereign ESG scoring and policy benchmarking; expect backlash over measurement.
A global grid-by-grid analysis (paper in review) finds South Asia stands out for coexistence of large wildlife with dense human populations, e.g. tigers and elephants in Coorg's coffee and smallholder landscapes, supported by Project Tiger and cultural reverence.
data_point🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: mediumSouth AsiaIndiaCoorgWestern GhatsProject Tiger
So what: Shows biodiversity outcomes are achievable in working, densely populated agricultural landscapes.
Malhi argues community-based nature recovery matters because it creates ecologically valuable mosaics of heterogeneity and restores local agency, complementing top-down international architecture and finance.
framework🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: mediumcommunity nature recovery
So what: Supports demand for local/bottom-up nature finance mechanisms alongside national incentive schemes.
Malhi says scientists should co-evolve relationships with policymakers and journalists early rather than handing over finished results; Nate Hagens adds he no longer believes the problem is an information deficit.
framework🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: mediumpolicyscience communication
So what: Policy shifts on nature follow relationship networks, not data releases.
The Natural History Museum of London's National Education Nature Park aims to get most schools and kindergartens in England to plan for increasing nature on their grounds with experiential learning.
data_point🗣 Yadvinder Malhiconviction: lowNatural History MuseumNational Education Nature ParkEngland
So what: Minor signal of institutional demand for school-ground greening and biodiversity monitoring.

Is Putin PLAYING Trump? | Ex-Ambassador Michael McFaul on NATO, Ukraine, Russia, and Zelenskyy47 min20 insights2026-11-25
▶ Watch on YouTube
Former US ambassador Michael McFaul argues Putin is an ideological, unconstrained autocrat pursuing imperial goals, so Trump's concession-heavy negotiating (aid cut to zero, NATO ruled out, diesel sanctions relief) has moved the war further from ending. He thinks the realistic route to a Korea-style frozen ceasefire is more weapons for Ukraine and more sanctions on Russian companies.

Putin's ideological calculus 6
Michael McFaul argues Putin is ideological rather than transactional, so deal-based Western negotiating frameworks (like Trump's 'Art of the Deal' approach) systematically misread him.
framework🗣 Michael McFaulconviction: highPutinTrumpRussia
So what: Peace-deal headlines premised on transactional bargaining with Moscow deserve skepticism; price lower odds of a negotiated end.
“I don't think Putin is transactional. Uh, I think he's very ideological, um, and motivated by a world view.” ▶ 2:02
McFaul says Putin's worldview rests on three beliefs: Russia is a civilization separate from Europe, the US is out to destroy it, and the former Russian Empire (including Ukraine) must be reunited.
framework🗣 Michael McFaulconviction: highPutinRussiaUkraine
So what: Implies Russian war aims are open-ended rather than bounded, sustaining long-duration geopolitical risk premia in European assets and energy.
By McFaul's count the war has cost Russia 1.2-1.5 million casualties for advances of only kilometers over three years, which makes no sense on cost-benefit terms but does on an imperial-mission view.
data_point🗣 Michael McFaulconviction: highRussiaPutin
So what: High attrition is not a reliable signal of imminent Russian capitulation; don't trade casualty headlines as ceasefire catalysts.
“1.2 1.5 million casualties depending on the numbers you believe to do what?” ▶ 3:15
McFaul says Putin answers to no one: Russia is now more autocratic than the late Soviet Union, the last major independent outlet (Novve Gazetta) has closed, and the last serious coup threat ended with the plotter killed.
thesis🗣 Michael McFaulconviction: highPutinRussiaNovve GazettaPogosian
So what: No internal elite or popular brake on the war means regime-change or palace-coup scenarios should be assigned low probability.
“You know the real answer is no one.” ▶ 0:04
McFaul contends autocracies can be resilient for a long time and Western observers overestimate how quickly discontent forces change in Russia.
contrarian🗣 Michael McFaulconviction: mediumRussia
So what: Fade 'Russia collapse' narratives as trading theses; assume regime continuity.
A senior Putin aide (Eager Shoubalof, then first deputy prime minister running the economy) told McFaul days before the 2014 invasion that Russia cares more about Ukraine than the US and has longer attention spans; McFaul thinks Putin may be right about American impatience.
anecdote🗣 Michael McFaulconviction: highEager ShoubalofPutinUkraineRussia
So what: Russia's strategy is to outlast Western attention; watch for US aid fatigue as the key variable rather than battlefield events.
“we have longer attention spans than you Americans” ▶ 32:33

thesis🗣 Michael McFaulconviction: highTrumpPutinUkraineNATO

Russian oligarch structure 4
McFaul splits Russian elites into private-sector oligarchs who wanted to expand abroad and hate the war, versus state-enterprise (oil and gas) controllers, mostly ex-KGB, who are ideologically aligned with and beholden to Putin.
framework🗣 Michael McFaulconviction: highRussiaPutinKGBoil and gas
So what: Sanctions pressure on private oligarchs won't move policy; leverage lies with state commodity exporters whose revenues fund the war.

contrarian🗣 Michael McFaulconviction: highRussiaPutin

“I think that's a big mistake we make analytically in the West because we think of rich people as being independent.” ▶ 15:32
Because oil and gas are global commodities that can be sold anywhere, Russia's state-run exporters do not need European or other finished-goods markets, a form of capitalism aligned with Putin's ideology.
causal🗣 Michael McFaulconviction: mediumRussiaoil and gas
So what: Commodity fungibility blunts market-access pressure; energy sanctions enforcement is what matters.
McFaul says Putin's rise was helped by luck: Russia's economy began growing around 2000 mostly because of rising oil and gas prices after a 1990s depression three times worse than the US 1930s, and he jailed Hardikoski in 2003 to discipline oligarchs.
data_point🗣 Michael McFaulconviction: mediumPutinHardikoskioil and gasRussia
So what: Regime popularity is tied to energy prices; lower oil and gas prices are a genuine pressure point on Moscow.

Poker as risk-taking framework 2
Jason Koon's tournament strategy: don't force action because the structure is fast; let spots come to you, but have the nerve to pull the trigger when they arrive, since some people wait too long.
framework🗣 Jason Koonconviction: mediumpoker strategy
So what: Directly transferable to position-taking: patience in selecting bets combined with decisiveness once edge appears.
“You can't try to create something out of thin air, you know?” ▶ 15:38
A viral clip claiming 1-in-32-million odds for calling all five board cards was actually closer to 1 in 300 for the flop and 1 in 2,500 for turn and river, showing how viral probability claims are routinely overstated.
data_point🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: lowprobability
So what: Reminder to recompute headline odds and statistics rather than accept viral framing.
“So, it wasn't like a 1 in 32 million like they said it was” ▶ 36:00

Adversity and immigrant founder edge 4
Dave Blundin cites data that while 14% of the US is foreign born and naturalized, 60% of tech CEOs by market cap are foreign born (e.g., Jensen Huang, Lisa Su, Sundar Pichai, Satya Nadella), making foreign-born people roughly eight times more likely to become a top tech CEO.
data_point🗣 Dave Blundinconviction: highJensenLisa SuSundarSatya Nadellatech CEOs
So what: Supports a founder-selection heuristic favoring immigrant founders; also flags immigration policy as a long-run risk factor for US tech leadership.
“60% of tech CEOs by market cap are foreign born.” ▶ 31:41
Dave Blundin says US-born college dropouts are about three times more likely and adopted people about ten times more likely to become top tech CEOs by market cap, concluding that adversity builds the grit that predicts success.
data_point🗣 Dave Blundinconviction: mediumtech CEOs
So what: A screening heuristic for early-stage investors: weight founder adversity and grit.
“anything that that makes you gritty, anything that's difficult in your life just makes you stronger and makes you more likely to succeed” ▶ 32:27

data_point🗣 Salim Ismailconviction: mediumGoogle

Venture lessons from founder near-misses 5
Dave Blundin recounts that his first company, Data Sage, nearly lost him the CEO job when an expected Amazon deal stalled (Walmart had sued Amazon and threatened its vendors, under NDA); once settled, Amazon as a reference customer drove a billion-dollar liquidity event less than 18 months later.
anecdote🗣 Dave Blundinconviction: highData SageAmazonWalmartJeff BezosRick Dalzell
So what: Marquee reference customers can re-rate an enterprise startup fast, but announcing deals to the board before close creates governance risk; outcomes hinge on randomness and timing.
“So I made the tragic Jensen Huang moment mistake of telling my board.” ▶ 11:25
Dave Blundin says his mother's small seed investment in his first company returned about 50x, at a time when no venture community would fund founders straight out of college.
data_point🗣 Dave Blundinconviction: highseed investingventure capital
So what: Illustrates the power-law returns of tiny pre-seed checks and how much more available early capital is today than then.
Peter Diamandis says the $10 million X Prize for private spaceflight was rejected by at least 150 potential sponsors (including Richard Branson twice and Paul Allen) over four and a half years before Anousheh Ansari, who had sold Telecom Technologies to Sonus Networks for $1.3 billion, funded it as the Ansari X Prize.
anecdote🗣 Peter Diamandisconviction: highX PrizeAnousheh AnsariSonus NetworksRichard BransonPaul Allen
So what: Incentive prizes (pay only on success) can catalyze new industries like private spaceflight, but funding them requires finding sponsors with idiosyncratic personal fit.
Peter Diamandis says his small-launcher company International Microspace targeted 100 lb to orbit for $1 million well before the small-satellite market existed, admitting he is typically about 20 years too early on many companies.
anecdote🗣 Peter Diamandisconviction: mediumInternational Microspacesmall satelliteslaunch
So what: Timing risk: correct theses can fail for being early; small-launch economics took decades to become viable.
“I've typically been 20 years too early on a lot of my companies.” ▶ 24:59
Alex Wissner-Gross says his rejection from an MIT AI research program circa 1998, during the AI winter, pushed him into nanotechnology, giving him cross-training at the physical-digital interface that now shapes his work as a researcher, entrepreneur and investor.
anecdote🗣 Alex Wissner-Grossconviction: mediumAI winternanotechnologyIntel Science Talent SearchMIT
So what: Signals the speaker's investment lens favors physical-world/digital-world intersections (robotics, nanotech, hardware) over pure software.

Non-market episode: Hasan Minhaj interviews children's YouTube creator Miss Rachel about developmentally grounded kids' music, the speech-therapy origins of her show, the Mr. Rogers / Sesame Street tradition, and her advocacy for children in Gaza, Sudan and ICE detention. YouTube kids' media economics 3
Hasan Minhaj frames YouTube as the dominant children's streaming service, but says most of its kids' content is low-quality, algorithmically generated material, so that YouTube had to turn off autoplay for kids.
thesis🗣 Hasan Minhaj (host)conviction: highYouTube
So what: Supports the view that kids' attention is consolidating on YouTube, with brand-safety and content-quality risk sitting with the platform.
“YouTube has become the preeeminent children's streaming [music] service, but it's mostly trash.” ▶ 0:01
Miss Rachel has about 20 million subscribers, and her 'Baby Learning with Miss Rachel' video has 2.4 billion views, more than the 1.9 billion views on Shakira's 'Hips Don't Lie'.
data_point🗣 Hasan Minhaj (host)conviction: highMiss RachelYouTubeShakira
So what: Shows how big a single educational creator can get on YouTube; one creator rivals top music catalog assets on views.
“Baby Learning with Miss Rachel, 2.4 billion views.” ▶ 6:38
Miss Rachel says she does not track her view counts and explicitly rejects pressure to grow her brand into an empire or franchise, saying money beyond 'enough' should be given away.
contrarian🗣 Miss Rachelconviction: highMiss Rachel
So what: Suggests that one of the largest kids' IP brands is unlikely to be heavily licensed or franchised under its creator's current stance.
“What sucks is people being like, "Make more and more money or like grow into an empire or become a franchise." Like that sucks.” ▶ 1:00:20
Developmental design of kids' content 2
Miss Rachel argues that repetitive, gesture-based kids' songs that adults find annoying (Baby Shark, Wheels on the Bus) help development, because gross-motor imitation and copying gestures support speech development.
framework🗣 Miss Rachelconviction: highBaby SharkWheels on the Bus
So what: A design principle that separates durable educational kids' content from novelty content, and is useful when judging kids' media IP.
Miss Rachel built her show partly to deliver speech-therapy techniques for free, after paying about $175 per 45-minute session, three times a week, for her son's severe speech delay. Some of that therapy moved to Zoom during the pandemic.
data_point🗣 Miss Rachelconviction: highMiss Rachel
So what: Points to high out-of-pocket costs and access gaps in pediatric speech therapy, which free video content is partly filling.
“I think it was $175 for 45 minutes.” ▶ 16:26
Advocacy and brand backlash 1
Miss Rachel has faced organized backlash for her advocacy on Gaza, Pride and ICE detention, including the group Stop Anti-Semitism nominating her for Anti-Semite of the Year and calls for a Department of Justice investigation. She says speaking out 'did cost us certain things' but she will keep doing it, and that the backlash had a chilling effect on others who might have spoken up.
anecdote🗣 Hasan Minhaj (host) / Miss Rachelconviction: highMiss RachelStop Anti-SemitismGazaDepartment of Justice
So what: One of the biggest kids' creators accepts that her political stances carry costs. Brand partners and platforms should expect the controversy to continue.
